NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the Rural District Council of Runcorn, being the Sanitary Authority for the Rural District of Runcorn, in the County of Chester, 3.ave, in pursuance of the Private Street Works Act, 1892, at a meeting held on the 24th day of September, 1912, by resolution approved of certain specifications, plans and sections, estimates and provisional apportionments, prepared by their Surveyor in respect of the following street:— SMITH STREET And certain Passages in the Parish of WALTON INFERIOR in the said District, and that such resolution is in the following terms, that is to say : (a) That the Rural District Council of Runcorn, being the Sanitary Authority for the Rural District of Runcorn, level, pave, metal, flag, channel, and make good the following streets or parts of streets and passages, in the Parish of Walton Inferior, in the said District, under the provisions of the Private Street Works Act, 1892, viz. : Smith Street throughout its existing extent as measured in a south-westerly direction from its junction with Chester New Road to a point 71 feet from the said junction. THREE LUCKY ENGLISHMEN. THE Statement that British Subjects never win any thing with Foreign Bonds has received another startling contradiction, for at tbo drawing of Hungarian oncier 1906 Bonds bold at Buda Pest on August 26 th,the FIRST PRIZE OF £18,500

, was won by a gentleman residing at Taunton; the Second Prize was secured by a Retired Sea Captain in Coma all ; and the third and fourth prizes both went to a gentleman residing at Bushy Heath. All these Bonds were sold by

CUNLIFFE, RUSSELL & CO., of 10 & 12, PLACE DE LA BOURSE, PARIS. The first-mentioned Bond had only been fully paid for in May last, and OD the second Bond several instal- ments still remained to be paid, whereby the assertion that their Customers frequently win Prizes during the time they are making their remittances receives new confirmation. Hungarian Foncier 1906 Bonds take part in six drawings each year at which drawings enormous sum of money are distributed—indeed in December 1910 another Client of Messrs. CUNLIFFE, RUSSELL tic CO. (also a British Subject) drew Forty-one thousand Two Hundred and Fifty Pounds sterling, which amount will again be drawn for next December.

NOTES BY " OYALIST."

The slaughter by Wigan did not materialise on Saturday. Warrington proved that they have the nucleus of a first-class side in their ranks by giving the all-world combination from Central Park an exceedingly hard game. Wigan won—unfortunately--- but it was only by seven points to five, and with the least bit of luck Warrington would at least have I finished on equal terms. Four of Wigan's points came from penalty kicks for off-side, and the deciding goal was kicked three minutes from the close of the game. The referee, Mr. Dickinson, was a bit inclined to favour the strong against the weak, and might have thought twice before giving the match into Saarrock's keeping almost on time.

The game was by far the best which has been seen at Wilderspool this season, and the pace which was maintained by both sides for the full 80 minutes was a tribute to their training and condition. There was some capital football and the improved form of the Wirepullers was most marked. They were inferior to Wigan in com- bination and resource, but they showed any amount of dash, and their defence was magnificent. What they lacked most was finishing power and at least two tries should have been scored before the interval.

The story of these two missed opportunities is the measure of Warrington's woe. So far as Wigan are concerned the defence was beaten, and the Warrington men had only themselves to blame for failing to gather in the much-desired points. In the first instance the ball had been carried to the line and Brooks had only to give Rushton a proper pass for the try to be scored. Unfortunately the pass was heaved a yard in front of Rushton and went to the ground. The second misfortune was a minute from the interval, when Wigan were leading by a try to nil. Dickenson was unmarked in front of the posts when Renwick threw him a long pass which he unaccountably dropped. If he had taken the ball he could have walked over! There was a groan from the crowd which nearly brought on darkness, and Dickenson looked as if he had lost his best friend. Plums like these two chances cannot be thrown away against a side like Wigan without retribution coming hot foot on the scene.

Apparently the Fates made up their 'mind to tantalise Warrington after this. Wigan did a lot of attacking in the second half, with the wind behind them, but all they got was a penalty goal. The Warrington defence near the line was rock like. Then came Warrington's turn. Young Tranter got hold at half-way and made a brilliant run which left Mclntyre with a clear opening. The wing man, who had been slow all afternoon, put in a great run and forced the ball over the goal line. Sharrock obstructed him badly and the referee awarded Mclntyre a legitimate try. In the meantime Brooks bad dashed up at a great pace and made doubly sure by dropping on the ball, but the try was Mclntyre's. Renwick equalised with the goal-kick,and the cheering could have been heard in Montenegro—almost.

There was only five minutes to go, and a division of the points seemed both assured and equitable. But Wigan made a great effort and succeeded. It happened this wise. A long punt was sent to Mclntyre near the corner-flag. The wing man had only to let the ball go dead and the game was over. But he foolishly punted out in the excitement of the moment, and as he did not follow up, the Warrington forwards were put cff-side. Sharrock appealed,the whistle went, and Wigan's burly full-back kicked a fine goal from a position straight in front of the posts. Thus Mclntyre undid all his good work in the previous five minutes, and presumably the gods smiled at Warrington's dis- comfiture. It was a, thrilling but unhappy finish. Comparing the teams, Wigan's great advantage was a two-fold one. The forwards heeled out better from the pack and the half-backs were streets ahead of the Warrington pair. It was at half-back where Warring- ton were let down. Rushton and Renwick failed to make openings for a three-quarter line which was every bit as good as Wigan's on the day's play. Until they get half-backs of the right stamp Warrington will not win matches. Renwick is clearly out of place, and Rushton is not class enough for the type of men he is meeting. Thomas and Gleave were perfect distributing agents. and if the Wigan centres had been up to the old standard Warrington would have been badly beaten. But the best centre on the field was Treater, who more than justified the good things said of him. He can run, feint, and pass with the best of them. With

NEXT WEEK : The Imps at Asssoclation Football.

Warrington are steadily improving. The to new Ebbw Vale forwards, R Thomas and Cox, both made a good impression and seem to be scrummagers of the right stamp. They are both strong, and clean-built young fellows. I hardly thought Ilevan seemed good enough for the senior team though he worked hard. George Thomas was the hero of the vanguard and his tackling was heroic at time. In the loose Skelhorn was always prominent. The pack will want some beating in. a week or two.

A determined effort is being made to remedy the deficiencies at half back, and on Saturday evening the Club concluded negotiations with Sidney Nicholas, the young half-back associated with the Tredegar Rugby Union Club. Nicholas, although only 20 years of age, has had a distinguished career in South Wales, and last week assisted Monmouth County at Abertillery against Gloucestershire, playing a really fine game. He was a spectator of the match between Warrington and Wigan, and expressed a desire to get into harness as soon as possible. He will play with the Combination team at Wilder- spool against Salford St. Bartholemew's this afternoon. Mr Ashton, the secretary of the Club, saw young Nicholas play against Gloucester, and has a high opinion of his abilities. But War- rington still require another half back. Meanwhile, Price, the Cumberland half, has gone to Dewsbury, and with Buckley gone to Wakefield, the supply,is getting limited.

great performance, and indicates that Warrington have still some good footballers in their ranks.

The first team are away to-day at Barrow, where they generally do well and where last season they on by 8 points to nil. I hope they will do as well to-day, but in view of Barrow's improved form they will have a stiff task. The selected side differs con- siderably from the one which did duty last Saturday, but I think it is good enough to win if the new men play up to their best form. Brooks is unwell, and Dickenson and Mclntyre have not been selected. This brings in Woodburn and Waywell, while Daintith is to have a trial at half with Rushton. He has had many opportunities, and if be had only cultivated running and passing in place of the punt, he would have been a regular first-teamer long ago.

The team is : Jolley, full back ; Waywell, Renwick, Tranter and Woodburn, threPquarters ; Rushton and Daintith, half backs; G. Thomas, R. Thomas, Cheaters, Cox, Skelhorn and Bevan, forwards.

The "A "team will probably have an easy journey at home against Salford St. Harts, and the presence of Nicholas at half back should be a big attraction. I wonder why Clare has been preferred to Twist. The Widnes lad has twice the promise as a footballer, and yet the committee keep persevering with Clare. His place is forward and nowhere else. Hudson was injured at Wigan and is having a week's rest, while Jordan goes on the wing. He has played in every position among the backs this season, and is the champion emergency man.

Warrington Extend the Wiganers. The popular engagement with Wigan at the Warring- ton Club's headquarters on Saturday drew the largest crowd of the season, some 7,000 people paying £166 for admission. Contrary to expectations the Wiganers did not bring more than a few hundred supporters, or the crowd would have been larger. Glorious summer-like weather prevailed, and with both players and conditions in fine order, a magnificent game was put up for the delectation of the crowd. Warrington did exceptionally well against their famous opponents and were unlucky to lose. The home side included for the first time, the two ex-Ebbw Vale forwards, Thomas and Cox, and both made a favourable debut. Teams and officials : Warrington—Jolley, back ; Brooks, Dickenson, Tranter and Mclntyre, threequarter-backs ; Renwick and Rushton,half-backs ; Chesters,G Thomas,Skelhorne, Bevan,R Thomas and Cox, forwards. • Wigan—Sharrock, back ; Todd, Price, Jenkins and Bradley, threequarter-backs ; J Thomas and Cleave, half-backs ; Seeling,WilliasnS, Silcock,Francis, Coldrick and Ramsdale, forwards. Referee, G F Dickinson, Halifax. Touch judges, J Longdin, Swinton, and J Valentine, Salford. Warriagton had the advantage of the breeze for the tint half, but for all that the Wiganers were the first to attack, Gleave, Thomas, Price and Bradley handling the ball at a rapid pace on the left wing, where Mclntyre checked operations near the home line. Wigan were very active in the loose, and a fine run by Seeling and Francis gave Jolley a warm handful, but the young custodian cleared splendidly. Some lofty punting by Renwick and Tranter cleared the invaders out and Tranter led a raid into the Wigan quarters by dribbling at a great pace over half the length of the field and com- pelling Sharrock to put in touch near his own line. A fine run by Bradley on the left was ended by Dickenson bringing off a grand tackle. Then the veteran centre broke through cleverly and passed to Brooks. The latter ran to the line and spoiled a glorious chance by passing forward to Rushton. Wigan were hard pressed to defend for the next ten minutes, the Warrington men playing with any amount of dash and breaking up all attempts at combination by Gleave and Thomas. Dickenson intercepted a Wigan pass and raced clear away, but failed to elude Sharrock under the posts. Wigan eventually rushed the game to centre, where Todd got hold and threw inside to Seeling. The tall Colonial went away at a fine pace, and when challenged by Jolley, threw back to Todd, who grounded at the corner just as he was knocked in touch. The try was safely made however, but Sharrock failed at goal. This was an unexpected ,reverse for Warrington, who had had the best of the play, and they soon had the ball near the Wigan line again. Renwick snapped up the oval in a melee close to the corner, and threw wide to Dickinson. The centre had only to take the ball to score under the bar, bt.t to the dismay of the home supporters he knocked on and the chance was lost. This was indeed a lucky ,escape for Wigan, who crossed over a minute later leading by a try to nil. With the wind behind them Wigan soon made pro- gress but met with a grand defence. Thomas tried the short kick from the pack but was well met by Renwick. Jenkins and Bradley got away but the wing man was bored irto touch and eventually Thomas and Skelhorn made a fine burst into the Wigan half. Todd was given a chance on the other side but would not face Brooks, and his kick was safely returned by Jolley. The Warring- tonians were penalised for obstruction ' and Thomas was called up to take the shot at goal, but it went wide of the mark. J Thomas again bluffed the opposition and Bradley ought most certainly to have put on the finishing touch, but he clean missed the ball. Warring- ton got into trouble for offside tactics, and this time Sharrock had no difficulty in placing a goal, for the position was opposite the posts. The locals went away directly to the Wigan line, and had the home half-backs been just a shade more discreet when within measur- able distance of the line the Wigan goal must have been captured. Fate was against them for the time being, and Seeling and Ramsdale effected a momentary clearance. A speedy respacsa was forthcoming, for Tranter got possession, and bluffing Bradley and Price handed on to Dickenson. Mclntyre made a rare burst and kicked over Sharrock's head. Being obstructed, there were loud cries against the infringement, but meanwhile Brooks bad raced up at a fine pace and dropped on the ball. Mr Dickinson consulted the linesman and awarded a try to Mclntyre for obstrec- tion. Renwick took the place, and, amidst great enthu- siasm, succeeded in making the scores level. Wigan were by no means done with, for they raided the War- rington quarters in the last few minutes left for play. Mclntyre fielded a punt on the goal line, and kicked to the open when he should have made the ball dead. The home forwards were all off side and again were Wigan awarded a penalty in front of the posts. Sharrock took the kick and landed another goal three minutes from the close,giving. his side a lucky victory. Final : Wigan, 2 goals, 1 try (7 points) ; Warrington, 1 goal, 1 try (5 points).

BRADFORD NORTHERN BEAT RUNCORN. Bradford Northern gained their second victory of the season by beating Runcorn at Birch-lane, Bradford, before about 5,000 spectators. The run of play in the first half was fairly even, the only score being made by Wilson after a neat passing movement, Craven goaling. Bradford did nearly all the attacking in the second half. Schofield crossed, followed by Wilson, and then in one of their few excursions into the home territory Runcorn scored through Thornton, Richardson improving. Steward, in following up a low kick, gained possession near the Runcorn line, and scored a try, to which Craven aided the goal points. Result : Bradford Northern, 2 goals. 4 tries (16 points); Runcorn, 1 goal, 1 try (5 points). Teams : Bradford Northern.—Steward, back ; Schofield, Hall, Wilby end Wilson, three-quarter backs ; Craven and Marsden, half-backs ; Winterburn, Haley, Stancliffe, Burrall, Feather and Mitchell, forwards. Runcorn—Shaw, back ; Mooran, Moores, Thornton, and Nev-all, three-quarter backs ; Steele and Sutton, half backs ' • Richardson. Peacock, Inman, Prince, Massey and Pad bury, forwards. Referee, C Platt, Swinton. ST. HELENS FAIL AT SWINTON. . •

The meeting of these Lancashire clubs at the Chorley- road enclosure attracted one of the best gates of the season, and they were rewarded by witnessing a fast and keen game from start to finish. The visitors handled far better than the home backs, but they lacked finish. In the opening half the visitors had slightly the better of matters, and when the interval was reached led by one penalty goal, placed by Turtill. It was in the second half that the home side asserted themselves, and they were put on level terms through a penalty goal, kicked by Ryder. They afterwards played with splendid clnsli, Moore running in a brilliant try from a pass by Fairhurst, Ryder adding the goal points. MOORE AND DARESBITRY V. PRESTON BROOK. The game at Moss-lane ground, Moore, on Saturday, was looked forward to as a local Derby Day. The rival *teams were Moore and Daresbury v Preston Brook. In .previous games there has often been a close struggle for 'mastery, but on Saturday the visitors were a thoroughly beaten team. At the onset of the game Preston Brook looked like being the aggressive side, but after a few 'PIaY minutes' the game settled into the visitors' half, where it remained for the greater portion of the game. The scorers for the home teim in the first half were M Cartwright. Hde Prez and T Wilkinson. In the second half the game was much the same as the first half, more gols being added for Moore and Daresbury by M Cartwright and F Ryder. Score :—Moore and Dares- bury, 5 goals ; Preston Brook, nil. WARRINGTON ALLIANCE LEAGUE. CADis HEAD 'V Sr. BARNABAS.—PIayed at Cadishead. The home team kicked off, and for the first ten minutes the visitors had a hot time. Play was transferred to the other end. Vernon, the outside left, put in a capital shot, which completely baffled the" Cads" custodian. Shortly afterwards Vernon scored again. Or a long time the respective defences held their own, bntil a movement by Challinor ended in his scoring. Half-time was reached in favour of the Saints. HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD.

The First Eleven ;173AEW —Wi,h-er-i—unexpected reverse at Bootle by 5-1. Without detracting from Bootle's Performance it should be said that Latchford made the Journey without some of their regular players ; the home team were also better accustomed to the Peculiarities of the ground. The Bootle forwards were quick and had a good nuderstanding with one another ; their shooting was also on the mark. The visiting forwards were disjointed and weak in the circle, and the halves were much below their usual standard. Fletcher, Billington, and Wilkinson were the best of the backs, While Dale was the only forward who played up to form. Bootle scored 4 times in the first half. Banks reduced the lead from a corner with a high shot but Bootle got through again and won easily. The Second Eleven broke the spell of ill-luck which has been over them and won 4-3 against Bowdon "B". Any side playing under the Bowdon colours can be relied upon to play skilful hockey and Saturday's game at Latchford was intensely interesting, first one side and then the other having the pull. Latchford obtaining the winning goal near time. The play in the second half was exciting and each goal had some narrow escapes The Latchford back division all round gave an improved display and the forwards were sharper in the circle. The goals were scored for the home team by Pearson (2) Colton (2) and were all good efforts. The Bowdon left wing was very tricky and indeed the forward line has a whole fully extended the Latchford defence. This victory over a good team should act as a tonic for the Second Eleven. ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIA,TION

(N.A. CENTRE).

More than the usual number were out last week end and again the set board remains unaltered. The Gowy, and Norton in particular, had a good number of members trying for it weigher but they were di-- appointed in both places. The weather is now better than we have had in the height of the season but the fish seem to be off feed in most places. Old bands say that the rank water that has been lying on the meadows and draining into the canals and rivers is the cause, making the fish feel sickly. There might be some- thing in this, but between the maggot and stone-bait season are not as many F pecimens weighed in as during the the time when these' baits are in full go. We have looked up records and find July, August and September are the best months for maggot fishing and January and February for stonebait. In October, November and December there are fewer recorded in the three months than in any of the other two. Still with the weather in our favour there is yet time to secure the first place in one or two columns on the board. Fancy a dace at Boz 4drs taking premier place! Now is the time when they are usually brought from the swims at Chester.

CHESTER. The Dee and canal had a few members on Satnrday and Sunday last trying to upset calculations on the board, but they returned without doing so. Dace up to 5 and 60z were creeled a roach up to 9 and 10oz were taken. hut nothing in the vuty of weighers was caught. The tide in he Dee was very suitable tor the day's sport, being a 12ft 61n tide. Still nothing has been put up.

_ NORTON. The canal here had a good tem visitors on Sunday. The conditions were all that could be wished for, yet the fish were not to be lured by the many baits used by our locals. Two or three was the average. Several crossed to the other water but with no better success. About half-a-dozen went with paste and bread to try to capture the specials yet to be won. They also returned home without being any nearer their object.

AIICKLE TRAFFORD. The Gowy bad a fair muster here. The Shaker end has been cleaned out and fish have not yet settled in their favourite haunts. Ihe water again was quite clear and had very little run on it. Several members were from Saturday at noon until Sunday evening without taking a fish. W Hewitt did about the best with six roach at the Shaker end, and Messrs Cook Ashton and Sherratt at Little Barrow had about the best catches in the upper reaches. Dace was the species taken. but for the number of anglers present the week-end here was of the poorest kind. A. S.

MR. ALF. MADDOCK.

The winner of the Royal Oak Branch Angling Club annual competition which recently took place amongst 42 of the members. Mr Alf Maddock, is one of the most successful anglers of Warrington. He won the Club Championship of the Warrington Angling Society in 1910, and has a large collection of prizes which he has won during his angling career. Mr G Tryner is the popular president of the club and the doner of the handsome bronzes and marble clock which Mr Maddock won.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
CROSS COUNTRY RUNNINO.

Warringtonians took little interest in the massed meet of the West Lancashire C C.A., which took place at Clock Face on Saturday last. Probably this was due to many preferring to have a short rest after the track 808800, prior to settling down for their annual Cross Country preparation, which plays such an important part in conditioning the athlete for the track in the following season. some athletes leave their Cross Country runs until after Christmas and it is really remarkable to note the large proportion of same who have to train very bard, at the back end of the surnrher to get amongst the prizes, these runners cannot have noted the fact that the men who have had a careful cross-country preparation generally maintain a high standard of form until quite late in the follow- ing track season when they slow up for a brief period before beginning anew their quest for the further improvement which up to a certain standard they almost invariably make. One hundred and fifty-one started from the Clock Pace Hotel. After covering about five miles nearly 50 lined up for the dash —ver the last mile and the prize given by the landlord was won by the veteran J T Rimmer. Only two Warringtonians took part but A Gregson sixth and F Shadwell eighth are evidently going to show improved form. Over 100 novices dashed the last mile and .1 B Preston, West Cheshire, in annexing the only prize proved himself above the ordinary novice. Only 5 Warrington lads started and Joe Hanson well up was ninth bud Isaac Sankey ran well to finish twelfth.

TO-DAY'S FIXTURES. Warrington A.C. have a two pack run from head- quarters at 3 o'clock prompt. Annual general meetings of the Northern bodies at Manchester to-night.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BISHOP OF LIVERPOOL ON MAN'S FIRST DUTY.

The Bishop of Liverpool preached on Sunday morn- ing, to a large congregation at St. Mark's Church, St. Helens, on behalf of the diocesan funds. He said that one great failure of the Christian Church was to reverse the commandment of the Gospel. Christ said that the first and great commandment was to love God, but the tendency of modern Christianity was to put man first. It was not difficult to see the reason for that reversal for it was comparatively easy to know and love our fellow men and infinitely more difficult to love an unseen God. Continuing, the preacher said that the Bishop of Liverpool's fund was started a few years ago to meet the spiritual needs of the diocese. Up to the end of 1911, in five years, they had raised £51,000, and that money had been the means of calling-out in the various localities where grants had been made the sum of 1:140,000. In all £190,000 had been raised through the fund and spent on Church work. St. Helens had received about £2,500. He was issuing a list of the Church needs of the diocese, and they were very many indeed. New churches, church balls, and vicarages must be built all over the diocese if the Church was to keep pace with the growing and shifting population, and £lO,OOO a year was needed.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
EGGS AT A CORNISH MEETING.

At Tywardreath (Cornell) on Wednesday, Richard Johns, baker, was bound over on a charge of inciting boys to disturb a Liberal meeting. Eggs were thrown, one hitting the chairman in the cheat and the other breaking on a speaker's eye.

IMPUTATIONS WITHDRAWN.

ANOTHER PHASE OF THE LEVERSON CASE.

A COUNTY COURT ACTION ABANDONED.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger K.C., mention was made of the adjourned action brought by Messre Miller and Company, jev,ellers, Sankey-street. against Mr and Mrs Henry Maddock, Padgate-lane, Warrington, for the return of a solid silver tray, an electro-plated kettle, and a gold keyless watch, or their value (£32 108) together with damages for their detention. Mr Arthur Browne appeared for the plaintiffs and Mr Borgia, barrister, Manchester (instructed by Mr T ti Steel, of Warrington) tot the defendants. Mr. Burgis said the case was one to which great publicity had been given, inasmuch as the suggestion was made that the defendants had received the goods which were claimed by the plaintiffs, knowing that they had been wrongfully come by. Those suggestions were made frequently in that Court and were repeated in the Police Court on October 3rd. It was then suggested by the plaintiffs that Mrs Maddock was a person who ought to be in the dock. His Honour: Was that the view expressed on behalf of the present plaintiffs ? Mr Burgis : Yes. His Honour : If the present plaintiffs thought she ought to have been in the dock, why didn't they take steps to place her there? Mr Burgis : They have not done that. His Honour: They have said she ought to be there? Mr Burgis : They have said that in this Court. His Honour : If that is so, it would be a reflection on themselves for not having performed an apparent duty. Mr Burgle, continuing, said the very day after the imputations on his client's character were made in the Police Court, they received a notice discontinuing the action. He should submit to the Court that that was not the proper course to take. Tacitly it was a with- drawal of those charges, but it was a private with- drawal. The charges were made publicly, and his friend , ought not to have withdrawn his action by discontinu- ance, but to have come into Court and honourably withdrawn the reflections against the defendant.

His Honour: Has he power to withdraw? Mr Burgis : When the matter has once come within the cognisance of the court, there is no such thing as discontinuance. We are entitled to judgment In our favour. We should not,however, have taken that point if my friend had done the honourable thing, come into court and said" We find "(as they did find from what transpired in the Police Court) "that we have made a mistake." They ought to have come into court and done their best to restore the character which they had attempted to take away from us. In answer to his Honour, Mr Burgis said the case against Mrs Leversou was dismissed by the magis- trates.

Mr Browne: It was held that no prima facie case had been made out.

Mr Burgis : I understand they stopped the case before the whole of the evidence had been given. Mr Browne : Nothing of the kind. Mr Burgis said Mr Browne was asked in the Police Court to withdraw any allegations against Mrs Maddock, but that course was not taken. He ought therefore to have taken the opportunity in the County Court that day of publicly withdrawing the charges. His Honour: Possibly he may take that course, if he submitted his case to the judgment of the magis- trates and they expressed the opinion that there was no prima facie case. Mr Burgis : I should be very glad if he would do so. His Honour : People may start a prosecution, not only in perfect good faith but in the belief that they I are discharging a duty as citizens. Then when their case is heard by an independent tribunal and the opinion expressed that there is not even a prima facie case,they may withdraw charges which they made not maliciouilly but thinking they were justified. That is simply in regard to Mrs Leverson, but se are not dealing with her case. We are dealing with the indirect effect of that on the case of Mrs Maddock. Mr Browne, I presume, would agree that even if Mrs Leverson were guilty, it does not necessarily follow that Mrs Maddock was guilty. I suppose he would also agree that if Mrs Leverson were not guilty and there was no case against her, there could be no case against Mrs Maddock. Mr Browne : That is my positiun. His Honour : Therefore, I should think that that being so, Mr Browne will think it is the proper thing to bow to the opinion expressed by the magistrates and withdraw charges which might have been made without any malice, in the first instance. Mr Browne said his learned friend had been misled. When the case was dismissed, it had been heard right to the end of the evidence for the prosecution. It was then found that certain bills on account had been given by the manager for his clients,whinh had not come within the knowledge of his employers or within his (Mr Browne's, knowledge and he had no right whatever to give them. The magistrates found,that they had received £ll3, accounts produced by Mrs Leverson whilst in custody being found to total that sum. The plaintiffs still had £lB9 worth of goods in the possession of the police, which had been bought by other persons The magistrates decided that in face of the accounts, no jury would convict end that a prima I facie case had not been made out. But his learned _

-- friend had not told his Honour what they did after- wards. They dhallowed the witnesses' allowances of Mrs Maddock and the other persons who had bought the articles. After the case was over they sent for him (Mr Browne) and an application was made by Mr Steel for the restoration of the goods which were the subject of the present action. On Mr Steel's advice, the defendants refused to bring those goods to the Police Station and they were only produced when he (Mr Browne) threatened to apply fora search warrant. The Chief Constable opposed the restoration of the goods to Mrs Maddock, as they bad been given into his custody and he (Mr Browne) then offered to come to terms, the Chairman having suggested that they should do so. Mr Burgis had not mentioned that in Messrs Hodgkinson's case, Mrs Leverson had previously been sent for trial. His Honour : That does not affect Mrs Maddock. Mr Browne: Yes, it does, because she is one of the persons who bought in Messrs Hodgkinson's goods. His Honour : But the magistrates, knowing all about Messrs Hodgkinson's case, still said there was no prima facie case against Mrs Maddock. Mr Browne: No, they did not. They found that there was no prima facie case against Mrs Leverson in Messrs Miller's case. His Honour : I mean inferentially against Mrs Maddock. If there was no case against Mrs Leverson there could be no case against Mrs Maddock. Mr Browne said that when the Chairman suggested a settlement, he (Mr Browne) gave notice that he would withdraw the present action and pay the taxed costs. Mr Steel suggested that he should withdraw all imputations, but he at first hesitated to do so. The Chief Constable, one of the proprietors of Messrs Miller and Co. and he (Mr Browne) then had a con- sultation and he afterwards said he would withdraw all imputations against Mrs Maddock in Messrs Miller's case. Mr Steel would not consent to that, but pressed for a withdrawal of the imputations not only in

I F your child is fretful, it is most likely that his stomach is out of order. Remember that Steedman's Powders are unrivalled for regulating the bowels. This medicine contains no poison or narcotic.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
that case but in Messrs Hodgkinson's case in which Mrs Leverson had been committed for trial and Mrs Maddock's expenses disallowed. His Honour: If you were willing to withdraw all imputations in respect of Messrs Miller's case then, I presume you are willing to do so now. Mr Browne: Most certainly, sir. His Honour : That is all that is asked. Mr Browne: In Messrs Miller's case only. His Honour: Oh yes, in Messrs Millers case only. The other case (Messrs liodgkinson's) has not been opened. What Mr Burgis has asked seems quite reason- able in view of the decision of the magistrates. It is that in this case of Miller v Maddock, without any reference to Hodgkinson's case, the insinuations and imputations against Mrs Madd.,ck should be entirely withdrawn. I understand you are willing to do that. Mr Browne: I have been willing all through. Discussion then took place with reference to the form of the settlement—whether the action should be merely discontinued or judgment given for the defendants. Mr Burgis said the attitude of the defendants with gard to the case had been the attitude that any honest person ought to take up. At the close of the last hear- jug his Honour suggested that there should be a settle- ment. The terms Mr Browne asked, however, were that the defendants should hand over the goods and pay eight guineas for his costs. That was not an offer that he (Mr Burgle) could advise his clients to accept. What they offered to do was to allow the defendants to re-purchase the goods at precisely the same figure that they (defendants) had paid for them and pay their costs. He thought that was a fair and honourable course which would commend itself to the Court. After the luncheon adjournment, Mr Burgis said the magistrates suggested, atter the Police Court hearing, that his clients should do something in the way of res‘oring the goods. Mr Browne : I thought we were only on the matter of whether the action should be withdrawn or judgment entered. His Honour : At the same time, some stress was laid by you on the alleged non-production of the goods. Mr Bur gis said they were willing to restore the goods but they attached one condition—that there should be a public withdrawal of the allegations against his clients. That was suggested by the Bench. Mr Browne in an undertone, said "I will withdraw" and walked out of the Court. Mr Browse : That is wilfully misleading my friend. Mr Burgh; said that as the allegations were disseminated very widely, they felt there should be a reasonable public withdrawal. The result was that on receiving the notice of discontinuance of the action, they felt they ought to come to Court and that there should be an opportunity of the public knowing that the allegations had been withdrawn. Mr Browne : I said openly to the Bench that I would withdraw all implications and the Bench quite agreed with what I suggested when I said I would withdraw the action and repay their taxed costs. Mr Burgis: There was no reference to it in the newspapers. Mr Browne : Because the reporters had left. Mr Burgis : And we were therefore unable to have publicity given to the withdrawal of the charges. After correspondence had been read, his Honour said he was not going to say that Mr Burgle was not quite reasonable in the course he had adopted. There was, however, the question of whether be (the Judge) was empowered to give judgment for the defendants.. The rule said that a plaintiff might discontinue an action and that all he need do was to give reasonable notice to the Registrar and to the opposing party. It might seem a little hard that insinuations might be made and at the last moment withdrawn, without the defendant having the redress of judgment in his favour. However, if the legislature did noty enable him to give that judgment be could not do so. Mr Browne had publicly withdrawn all imputations in that case against Mrs Maddock and that was the great thing Mr Burgis was anxious to secure. Mr Burgis: If we had had the publicity before we should not have gone any further with the case. His Honour then assented to the discontinuance of the action and gave costs for the defendant up to the data of the receipt of the notice. A similar action brought by Messrs Hodgkinson was then mentioned.. It was stated that Mr. Leigh, counsel for the plaintiffs, was ill in Germany and as he was familiar with all the details of the case, an adjournment was desirable. Mr Burgis said it was possible that his friend might obtain a conviction against Mrs Leverson in the mean- time, and all he asked was that the plaintiffs should not make use of any new facts of that kind at the adjourned bearing. His Honour: That might be advanced as an argu- ment against any adjournment, because you cannot tell what the future may yield. Mr Browne: I understand from the Chief Constable that other charges will be preferred by him, in which Mrs Maddock will be a witness. His Honour : Are you putting these facts forward as grounds for an adjournment ? Mr Browne: No, on the ground that I ought not to have to pay the costs of the adjournment. It is unreasonable to ask me to do so. His Honour granted an adjournment until November 7th. the coats occasioned thereby to be paid by the plaintiffs.

PENALTY OF THE BRINE.

Stockton Heath Lady Summoned at

Northwich.

• 'Before the Northwich Magistrates on Tuesday, Mary Elizabeth Joynson, Stockton Heath, the owner, and Martin Connealey, the occupier, of extensive furnish- ing premises in Witton-street, Northwich, was summoned to show cause why the premises should not be demolished as ruinous and dangerous to passengers. Mr. J. A. Cowley,clerk to the Urban Council, said it was one of those most unfortunate cases which the owners in the district had to put up with. The Council felt most sympathetic towards owner and tenant, and did not come to court in any unreasonable spirit. The building bad become dangerous, and the surveyor declined to take any responsibility with regard to it. 1 Mr E J Fletcher, solicitor for the occupier, said he did not want to go into any controversial details, but his client did not want the building pulled about his ears without being given a chance of getting out and clearing his stock. He suggested that the building was no more dangerous than it had been at any time during the past two years, but the tenant was prepared to further support it as a temporary expedient. The Chairman: Anybody with any eyes can see it is extremely dangerous to anybody passing. and after that morning the responsibility would rest upon the bench. lhe Surveyor said that to make the premises safe he roughly estimated that it would cost £250 to £3OO. Mr Fletcher: Who would be injured by an adjourn- ment? The Chairman: The party a brick happens to fall upon. Mr Fletcher: It would be Mr Connesley who would be damaged, and that would be his own look out. It is astonishing how these places stick together in this district. The bench consented to a fortnight's adjournment on condition that without another moment's delay steps should be taken to satisfactorily prop the front of the premises and to demolish the dangerous wall at the back.

FERTILISERS IN A WET SEASON.

Mr. Ernest Parke has issued his eleventh annual report on the effect of fertilisers on the grass land of the naturally poor clay farm which be cultivates at Kinetom in Warwickshire. The season at Kineton, as elsewhere, was exceptional, by reason both of the early drought and the late rain. Mr. Parke's grass survived the drought well, and made a heavy trowth, but the hay was doomed to lie on the ground for some three weeks between cutting and 'carrying. Although its quality naturally suffered during this interval, the weights obtained from the different plots shot the quantity to have been good, and the effects of the various fertil- isers as shown by the scale are not less striking than during the previous ten years. In the two experimental grass-fields the unmanured plots this season averaged about 15 cwt. per acre of hay. The plots annually manured with phosphates alone (3 cwt. of superphos- phate per acre in the one field and 5 cwt. of basic slag per acre in the other) gave respectively 32 and .3S cat. of hay per acre, these yields being increased to 41 and 47 cwt. per acre by the addition of a spring dressing of nitrate of soda at the rate of P., cwt. per acre. The average increase of hay shown on the fully manured plots as competed wsth the unmanured land was approximately a ton and a half per acre in return for an expenditure of less then 30/-in fertilisers. Dr. Dyer, who advises Mr. Parke in his manuring, again reports that the best and most luxuriant mixture of clovers and grasses is found in the heavily yielding plots receiving both nitrate and phosphates as compared with the plots which receive either fertiliser separately.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRINCE OF WALES AS AN UNDERGRADUATE.

Dow times have changed and bow we have changed in them is strikingly illustrated by a comparison between the education of Edward VII. and that of hi• grandson, the Prince of Wales, who on Thursday entered Magdalen College, Oxford, as a freshman. Acting according to the tradition prevalent at the time, Queen Victoria surrounded her son and heir with all sorts of restraints and regulations. His companions were picked for him, his studies most carefully map?ed out and ' every care taken that he should be as much as possible wader the surveillance of his tutors. King George with his son has pursued a far more liberal policy, and one more in keeping with the times. The Prince of Wales, as tar as possible, will live the life of every other student at Oxford. He will chose his own friends, dine in hall, follow the pastimes that appeal to him and as far as possible, be treated exactly like an ordinary undergraduate. This is a very healthy change. The time has long gone past when it was considered possible to train a youth up to virtue by surrounding him with barriers that were not the less restraining because they were sometimes invisible.—" Country Life."

There have been "SOUND AND rumours of stirring FURY." events in the House of Commons. On Thurs— day the Prime Minister introduced his proposals for the reasonable restriction of debate on the Home Rule Bill, and Mr. Bonar Law moved an amendment which presumed that we are living under a condition of things in which the Constitution is no longer operative. The amendment provided the Government with the fine majority of 91. Certain of defeat, the Opposition has been talking about demonstrations which, it is believed, must greatly impress the mind of the country. According to one plan, the whole of the Opposition is to march out of the House of Commons when the closure resolutions are carried. According to another only the Ulster members are to march out. We do not believe that either of these courses will be followed. They might impress the mind of the country, it is true; but they would be likely to impress it very unfavourably. The country must have had nearly enough of demonstrations which are anything but complimentary to its common-sense. Most certainly it declines to be roused up into any sort of furore against Home Rule. Time has settled that question. "The stars in their courses are fighting on our side," said Mr Gladstone, in concluding one of the greatest of his Home Rule speeches. The stars have fought very surely and effectively. It is by this time understood that the claim of Home Rule for Ireland is one that no Government,

===== 0002601_19121012_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
1

Liberal or Conservative, could withstand; and theatrical displays in the House of Commons, will be as futile as the recent melodrama in Ireland, to drive the mind of the country from the conviction that the time has come for settling this question once for all. Does anybody believe that the Government is giving anything short of ample time for the remaining discussions on the Home Rule Bill? That measure will have been discussed for more than fifty days before it goes to the House of Lords. Every essential point can be discussed over and over again before the Bill leaves the House of Commons for the Lords. We admit that there will be ma". more or less essential points that will not be discussed, though power will be given to the Speaker to rule out what is not called essential. But this w,ill be because the policy of the Opposition is not to promote, but to obstruct, discussion. All that can be ssid against the Dill has been said already rnaniYi times over. The Opposition has proved itsel to be singularly barren in argument. was proved on several occasions during the early part of the session, it cannot eve° maintain a debate, and yet it is quite certai° that no arrangement proposed by the Prillie Minister would satisfy its demand for al allowance of time, which, if it was shorteae' to halt the present proposals, Conservative members could not fill up without conga° repetition of themselves and of each other. The Opposition is being inspired to actie° by a publication whieh, as the Solicitor-0°3.: eral said not long ago, "gives us an epilePtly fit once a day," It is worth while to tal‘e note of the sort of thing that is being applied as a stimulus to Conservative ree,o. bers of Parliament. They are told that Xr i Bonar Law's amendment is a step in a gre3 struggle for Constitutional liberty; that th° Government has abolished the count.: power of controlling its own destiny; that I has freed itself from interference bY the people; that the resolutions of the Goverrl- ment abolish the House of Commons as deliberative assembly; that Parliament ls being strangled, that the Parliament .15.°! proclaims the despotism of an unprinciPleu 'combine," and that the country is not to b,e, allowed even to "hear the arguments against the description of a kingdom and thf enslavement of a Province. Now we do "° ask whether anybody believes such State; ments as these to be true, being convince; that nobody does entertain that belief. 13° undoubtedly they are the ground-plan of the policy of the present Opposition, which convinced of nothing so much as that 01 country can be hood-winked by violel39 ' phrases and nonsensical assertions. It Iles once said of an eminent politician that he vie° a weak man with a strong style. rrhi „ present Opposition in ?arliament has 0 double disqualification. Not even its etYlf - j is strong, though it employs abundance what the late Poet-Laureate called of little meaning."

Not many days ago the FOOD TAXATION Berlin 'correspondent of IN GERMANY. a London Protectionist paper (the "Morning Post ') said about the condition of food- Xing Germany that "there is apparently ittle reason to doubt the genuineness of the distress which affects large bodies of the •Yv"orking-classes, and makes it practically Possible for them to purchase better kinds pf meat." The same correspondent quoted a leading Socialist as having "expressed his conviction that, were a General Election to take place this autumn, the four and a quarter million Socialist votes of last winter Would be increased to five millions." The 1-Torman Socialists held a Congress recently in Saxony, and passed a resolution: "Demanding the setting aside of the policy of Protection, of the system of importation certificates, and of the taxes , on corn and forage, and advocating admittance of cattle and meat into the country and the importation of fresh, and preserved meat, especially frozen meat."—Reuter message, published , September 18th. "How the ratepayers' money is spent" would be an appropriate title for the annual abstract' of the• Corporation accounts just issued by the Borough Treasurer (Mr. John Fairhurst). It is a volume of 171 pages and gives a mass of information as to the work- ing of all the Corporation departments. War- rington's indebtedness, it appears, stands at £1,343,593 but there is no danger of insolvency as the excess of assets amounts to £515.205 18s. 9d., exclusive of various valuable gifts and properties purchased out of revenue. *

The President of the Lancashire and Cheshire Licensed Victuallers, in his speech at the conference at Warrington on Thurs- day, made it clear that the Tory party was expected to "to toe the line '• to its old allies "the trade." The licensed victuallers, he said, had been hewers of wood and drawers of water for the Conservative party for a long time, and they wanted some definite information as to the rewards they might expect in due course. Humiliating, surely, for the party which is so fond of taunting the Government about Irish domination!

Should Members of Parliament be fined for absenting themselves from their duties ? Seeing that they now get £4OO a year, Sir Charles Hunterhas given notice of a question to the Prime Minister asking " whether he proposes to leave it in the absolute discretion of members to give or withhold their services to the House for which the salary is paid." A referendum on the point would probably turn out awkwardly for the M.P.'s. Just imagine the joy of the artisan voter when he read in the morning paper that his local M.P. had been "docked for losing a quarter! "

The question will, however, require a serious answer.

* - Ulster men who really do read for ti em- selves will be surprised to see how meekly the roaring lions of Portadown and New- townards have conducted themselves in Parliament this week. Of civil war there has been scarcely a word and even the Covenant was practically ignored. A huge shout of laughter greeted the sublime state- ment of Mr. Bonar Law that "Ulster is only quiet because she is restrained by the wise leadership of my right hon. friend "—Sir Edward Carson. Even the erstwhile King of Ulster shook with merriment. But in Belfast, men may well begin to wonder if they are not being made party puppets for Messrs. Bonar Law, Carson and Smith.

Miss Cornall's Concert. We would remind our readers that the matinee concert arranged by Mies Mary Gornall will be held in the Bold-street schodro m, on Thursday next The blowing artistes will take part : —Mrs Norman Melland, sop, ano ; Miss Amethe I.eadbetter, vitlinist; and Miss Evelyn Hall, harpist. Miss Leadbetter played with great success at Miss Gornall's concert last year. Wilhelmy the celebrated violinist wrote to her :—Miss Amethe Leadbetter is born to be a violin player and I consider her one of the greatest of the day." Miss Evelyn Hall is the sister rif Miss Mar it Hall the celebrated violinist and studied in Vienna from Professor Holy at the Royal Opera House. Funeral of Miss Jane Atkinson.

The funeral of the late Miss Jane Atkinson, who died on the previous We Inesday night, took place in St Thomas's Churchyard, Stockton Heath, on Saturday afternoon. The decaeed lady was well known in the parish of Christ Church, Latchford, where she hadl peeti prominently connected with parochial under- takings. She was especially interested in the Sunday school, where, es a teacher, she had given her services for the long/ term of 27 years. Two years ago she received a certificate from the Bishop of Chester in recognition of 25 years' faithful eervice in that direction. The Vicar of Christ Church (the Rev G C Briggs) read the first part of the burial service, and Mr T L Fry played the Dead March in " Saul" There was a good attendance. At the graveside the Rev G C Briggs again officiated.- The chief mourners were Messrs H Atkinson (brother), J Wilkinson, D F Wallington. T L Fry and W Starkey. There were numerous floral tributes. At the conclusion of the evening service at Christ Church on Sunday the organist played a funeral march. Sequel to a Carting Fatality At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, K.C., a case was down for hearing in which Mrs Lucy Agnes Eckersley, 31, Robson-street,widow of the late William Eckersley sued Messrs John Buckley and Co., Ltd., carting con- tractors, Church-street, for compensation for the loss of her husband. Mr T S Steel was for the applicant and Mr A J Willett for the respondents.—Mr Steel explained that on July 22nd the deceased was carting cinders from the Corporation electricity works to the bridge then in course of erection at Howley. He had emptied a load and had got into the cart to return to the cvorks,when he fell backwards on to the wheel, his spine being fractured. He was taken to the Infirmary where he died on August 3rd, leaving a widow and six children. The-respondents disputed liability on the ground that deceased was drunk and had been warned to cease working, prior to the accident, Negotiations had, hoicever, taken place and it had been agreed that there should be an award of £152 with agreed costs.—His Honour entered judgment accordingly. ' Wycliffe Church. The harvest thanksgiving services take place to-morrow (Sunday), when the preacher will be the Rev. J E Flower, M.A., of London, secretary of the Home Missionary Society. Special hymns will be sung, and during each service anthems will be rendered by the choir. Advantage has been taken to hold a great public meeting on Tuesday evening next. The opportunity to do this is caused through the autumnal meetings of the Congregational Union ,of England and Wales being held in the neigh- bouring city of Manchester. Sir Arthur A. Haworth, Bart., has consented to preside, and as this is his first public appearance in War- rington, doubtless many will desire to hear him The speakers will include the Rev A C Hill, of London, who possesses a very high reputation in the denomination, while of the Rev G McLuckie it need only be said that he was invited to succed Dr Jowett at Carr's-lane, Birmingham, but preferred to remain, at Neat Yarmouth. Additional interest is occasioned through the re-appearance in his old church of the Rev John Yonge, whose presence will give considerable pleasure to his admirers. Noncon- formists generally are invited and expected in large numbers, though, as the advertisement indicates, all are welcome. Royal Court Theatre. It will be welcome news to Warrington playgoers that the Lodge Percy Repertory Company is paying a return visit, after a successful tour in Ireland, and we congratulate the management of the Coutt Theatre upon including, in their autumn bookings, this famout stcek company. Miss Henrietta Schrier appears in the cast and this of itself is sufficient to ensure success, although we know Mr. Lodge-Percy to be an old and experienced touting manager and one to be depended upon to present his fare in the best possible fashion. Four plays will be presented next week in the following order: On Monday, Thursday and Matinee " Called Back," on Tuesday and Wednesday "An Unpardonable Sin," on Friday," The Sorrows of Satan," and on Saturday "East Lynne." Mr. Lodge Percy and his company won golden opinions upon the occasion of his visit last year and we have no doubt but that he will again receive generous support. Public feeling is setting strongly in favour of repertory companies, which of necessity, in order to meet the exacting demands of several plays, have to be com- posed of capable and accomplished artistes and we trust that Mr. Lodge Percy's bold experiment will meet with the success his efforts so well merit.

Presentation to the Rey. M. Rowley An interesting presentation took place on Sunday evening last in the Sacred Heart schools, Liverpool- road, when the Rev Father II Dowley was made the recipient of handsome presents including a massive and valuable canteen of cutlery. The Rector, Father W 11ckliff presided, and was supported by Fathers J Murphy, T 0 Swarbreck, 0.8. B„ P L Bugging, 0 S.B , Councillor Owen Toole, Messrs A M Crowe, J F Dolan, John Dolan, F Flanagan, T J Newns, G Coggins, J Smith, J Fay, J E Newns, etc. The room was crowded and many people were unable to obtain admission. The Rev Chairman paid a high tribute to the work Father Dowley accomplished as curate, and said the crowded meeting emphasised the respect in which he was held. Personally he was very sorry to lose him and be trusted he would be happy in his labours in his native diocese of Waterford. The presentation was made by Mr A M Crowe, who asked Father Dowley to accept these small tributes of esteem and affection in which he was held in War- rington, not for their value but as material expression of their feelings. The speaker in referring to the spiritual good which Father Dowley had accomplished in the town, /aid special stress on the great missionary work which the Irish clergy had done throughout the world and notably in English-speak- jog countries. They were sorry that the Rev Father was recalled to Waterford, but they assured him that he would always be affectionately remembered in Warrington. Fathers Murphy, Bugging and Swar- brook, Councillor Toole and Mr 3 F Dolan supported. Father Dowley feelingly responded, and agged them to accept his heartfelt thanks. He would have many happy recollections of the Sacred Heart, Warrington. Votes of thanks to the Chairman, artistes and the hon. secretary Mr J F Dolan, brought the proceedings to a close. Au excellent programme of music was contributed by Master L McGeer, Miss Marie Warren and Mrs John Dolan, Miss M Warren and Miss A Richardson were the accompanists.

LOCAL PARS.

HOOP for a Local Art pastor. ' We are pleased to announce that the National Society of Art Masters (of which the King is the patron) at a committee meeting on Saturday elected Afr George Shawcross Pendlebury, senior assistant at the Warrington School of Art, to an associate mastet- ship.This entitles him to append the letters A.N.S.A.M to his name. The honour only fall.s to fully qualified art masters who have gainea distinction in tie art world and we understand that Mr Pendlebury is the first Warringtonian upon whom it has been conferred. Mr Pendlebury was a pupil of the late Mr J Christmas Thompson, formerly headmaster at the School of Art, and won a free scholarship at the National Art Training School and Royal College of Art, London. This was. continued for eight consecutive years. During his training, and on the recommenda- tion of the Friucipal, Mr Pendlebury undertook relief duty at the Schools of Art at Chelsea, Putney, Canter- bury and Hertford, in the absence of the headmasters. He was afterwards appointed one of the official examiners to the Board of Education—a Position which he held for eight years. Then came his present appointment as senior assistant master at the Warrington School of Art. Mr Pendlebury is a medallist and holds the art master's certificate for group I. and 11. and parts of 111. Mr Pendlebury's many friends-will congratulate him upon the well- merited distinction he has now obtained, which must also be gratifying to the Education Committee in whose service he has worked so loyally and efficiently.

At the Padgate Stocks Hotel on Saturday last, a farewell party was given to Mr Edward Riley and Mr James Sudell (junr.), who depart for Australia on the 17th inst.—After a splendid repast, excellently served by the hostess (Mrs Fryer), the Chairman (Mr F C Gill) in a few preliminary remarks, said how sorry they were at losirg their two workmates, but hoped that it was the beginning of a new and better life for them, and wished them in the name of the company a pleasant voyage and good fortune.—Mr John Kay presented each with a pocket wallet and a gold scarf- pin. After the toast of the evening Our departing friends," had been honoured, a long and interesting programme of songs and dramatic recitals was most ably rendered by Messrs W Bridge, A Riley, J Prescott, G Mitchell, Mr McHugh and /0 Wolfenden.— A vote of thanks to the Hostess and Chairman terminated a long evening of pleasure and enjoyment. Assurance Agents' Meeting. In connection with the Warrington and District Superintendents' and Managers' " Fraternal Associa- tion," a meeting of insurance workers in Warrington and district was held in the Friars' Green school- room, on Thursday evening. Mr P Delaney presided over a good attendance. The meeting was called to consider the question of direct representation of insurance workers on the Town Council and after discussion it was decided to form a committee to take definite steps ti secure such representation. The corntni,tee will meet on Monday to decide whether to bring forward a candidate this year or to defer action until the following Nuvember. Interesting addresses were delivered by the Chairman, Messrs Storey, Forster, E Wynne Jones, Br( cklehurst aud A Edes (hon. secretary). Mr Brocklehurst said an industrial company afforded every facility for a: man to make provision Lir his family in case of death. It, was the wotk of such companies that caught thr eye of the Chancellor who then brought about the great National Health Insur- ance scheme with such success, entrusting its administration to industrial companies and friendly ercieties.

Death of Miss Hetherington. We legrPt ti have to announce the death of Miss Josephine E. Hetherington, late Head-mistress of the Beamout Infants' Council School, which took place on Runday last, at the residence of her sister, Priory- road, Anfield For some time Miss Hetherington had been in indifferent, health, but she only ceased active duties at the school about two months ago Miss Hetherington became a qualified teacher in 1t69, and after holding the position of headmistress in Rochdale, Doncaster and Colne, she commenced her duties at the Warrington Cockhedge British School in January, 1881. On the closing of that school in 1894 ehe was transferred to the St. George's Mission infants' school, where she remained as beadmistrees until the opening of the Beamont Council school in 1907. During the long period of 4g years Miss Hetherington had seen many changes in the methods of teaching in the public elementary schools, but she ts always ready to pick out the best of new proposals 'a a d adopt them in the interests of her scholars. Her thoughts were always directed to the welfare of those she called "my little ones." There are many men and women to-day in Warr ington who can look back and thank Miss Hetherington for many kindly actions. She was always thonghtful and considerate for the members of her staff and for all those with whom she was brought into contact, and although for many years she worked i ,under the most depressing conditions she never complained. Quiet and retiring by nature, Miss Hetherington lived in her school and for her school with a faithful devotion, and it can be said of her, as of many others in her most honourable profession .5' Well clone thou good and faithful servant."—The funeral'took place at the Anfield Cemetery, Liverp-rol, on Tuesday afternoon. In addition to the relatives, Canon Stevenson represented the ' managers of the tchool and the Education Committee, Mr W Mason, headmaster of the boys' department of the Beamont lachool, represented the Director of Education and the school staffs, and Mr S M Crosbie represented the head teachers of Liverpool. Wreaths were sent from the scholars of the Beamont infants' school, the staffs Qi the boys' girls', and infants' departments of the eamont school, Miss Bullen (Cairo-street Council 'school), Mrs Smith, and other Warrington friends. During the day the flag at the Beatnont school was flown at half-mast.

WHERE THE NAME COUNTS. The immense consumption of jams and marmalade a at the present title is a matter of more importance tian may at first sight appear, for the fact is that there is a great deal of adulteration and faking, and no one who wishes to eat only clean food should accept without discrimination any jam or raarmalade offered by the grocer. It is pleabant to be able to call attntion to 1 ono firm which sets a most excellent example in the matter of purity of ingredients, clean- liness of production, and in the just treat- ment of employees. Hartley's preserves are made under excellent conditions from freshly gathered fruit and high-grade sugar, and they have a clean flavour which no clever adulteration can hope to pro- duce. Both the marmalades and the various fruit preserves are made when the particular fruit is in 13"8" ; there is no passe in the process once the fruit Is nicked—it passes straight into the factory, and the finished product remains in the jars under airtight covers until it reaches the consumer. Messrs. Hartley pay high wages compared with those often paid in jam factories ; they have a profit-sharing scheme in full 'operation, and also a pension scheme. So that there are various perfectly good reasons why the readers of this paper should, when buying jem or marmalade, ask for Hartley's,

THE NOVEMBER ELECTIONS.

, With the approach of November, great activity is being shown by the political parties and keen fights for municipal honours are promised. The Liberals are making efforts to place a progressive candidate in the field in every ward and to change- the complexion of the Council which is at present too pronouncedly Tory. RETIREMENT OF COUNCILLOR OWEN TOOLE.

We regret to announce that Councillor Owen Toole is not seeking re-election in Bewsey Ward at the forth- coming November elections. Councillor Toole has for some time made Liverpool the centre of hie business and continued pressure has at last compelled him to arrange to reside nearer to that city. To the Irish and Catholic community in Warringtm his departure is a severe loss and his place will be very hard to fill. He is a Warringtouian and was born some 39 years ago in Ryland—street. From an early age he has been honourably and prominently connected with religious, political and social life in Warrington. Many and varied have been the offices which he has held and we need only mention that Councillor Toole has fulfilled tliti position of President of the League of the Cross, the Irish National League, United Irish League, the Catholic Association and the Catholic Y.M. Society, to show that his name has been synonymous with Catholic and Irish affairs for many years. He has been the local representative at Catholic Conferences and National Conventions held both in Great Britain arid Ireland.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR-10WEN TOOLE.

Councillor Toole has always been a source of strength to his co-religionists in the town, and his efforts during the Elucation crisis are not forgotten. He has always been a progressive democrat, and has been ever to the fore in championing the rights of the masses. He served on the following committees : Education, Praniways and Electricity, Markets, Paving and Sewering, Fire Engine, and General Purposes Committee. The people of Bewsey Ward will, we are sure, be sorry to lose one who has worked so hard during the distress of the last coal strike, and the Ward Com- mittee of which he was the chairman especially so. Councillor Toole has taken a great interest in the Parlianientory Debating Society and was for several sessions Chairman of the Nationalist group in our "local Parliament." In Bewsey Ward Mr S Gough and Mr A C Binks have announced their:intention of standing as Indepen- dents, and the Liberal Committee will meet at an early date to consider their course of action.

MR. PETER PEACOCK FOR WHITECROSS

WARD.

In Whitecross Ward the Liberals have an excellent candidate in Mr Peter Peacock, whose business experience, his valuable work on behalf of friendly societies, and his interest in the well-being of the working classes eminently fit him to represent this large and important watd. In his addre.s Mr Peacock states that although always an advocate of the best possible education for the masses, he is strongly in favour of the children being permitted, in necessitous cases, to leave school before attaining the age of. 14 years. School accommodation in the Ward is strictly 'limited, and he would therefore urge a speedy com- mencement of the contemplated new school in Lovely-lane. He would make it his immediate business to urge forward some scheme of connecting Sankey and Longford routes rra Lovely-lane (either by motor 'bus or tramway) as the great development of this portion of the Ward demands some such service, and he is firmly convinced it would be patronised to such an extent as would obviate it ever becoming a burden on the rates. In connection with any such scheme the question of halfpenny fares for intermediate stages would—as on previous occasions when in the Council—receive his utmost support. The question of the public health is always one of vital importance, and tiny suggestion for the improve- ment thereof, together with tbe opening out of recrea- tion grounds bath for children and adults would receive Mr. Peacock's ardent support. As a prominent Friendly Society worker the provisions of the Insur- ance Act as it affects the Town C,Juricil would natur- ally claim his particular attention. Councillor !Seinen Retires.

Councillor W. H. BeHien, who won the seat br the Conservatives three years ago, has decided not to offer himself for re-election. The Conservatives have not yet selected another candidate.

ST. AUSTIN'S WARD.

General satisfaction has been expressed that Mr. Edmund Osborne is contesting this ward as an Independent. He believes that business ability and not party colour should determine the electiLn of repre- iiientativea on municipal bodies and those who know him are convinced that he would bring shrewd judgment and broad sympathies, as well as an excellent training, to bear in the discharge of the duties of a councillor. Mr Osborne has proved his qualifications by excellent work on behalf of many local organizations and he is one of the most ppular members of the_committee of the Warrington Foot- ball Club.

ORFORD WARD.

Councillor Cunnington, the popular representative of this ward, will again seek re-election. He not tally takes the keenest interest in every question affecting the dwellers in Orford Ward in particular but keeps close watch on public expenditure .and always uses his influence on behalf of the workers. He is doing very useful work as chairman of till Gymnasium Committee. In his address Mr. Cunnington says be is a firm believer in the child of to-day being better cared for by local authorities. Whilst the parents should perform their share in the up-bringing of the children, looal authorities should assist by providing open spaces, healthy housing accommodation, clean and well lighted streets, aad wherever possible, open-air swimming baths.

LATCHFORD WARD.

In this ward Mr E Wynne Jones is fighting the Liberal battle and he would undoubtedly be an acquisition to the Council. His knowledge of public affairs has fsequently been shown on the platform; he is a resident in the ward and the ratepayers could rely upon their needs being voiced and pressed forward by him. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The Great Aerial Smiths are at the top of the tree in aerial gymnastics and present a turn at the Warrington Hippodrome this week which is remark- able for its daring and originality. They consist of one couple, lady and gentlemen, and perform compli- cated evolutions up in the "flies " with a rapidity and certainty which is marvellous. Their flying ttapeze work brings down the house; in fact it evokes shrieks of terror from the feminine portions of the audience when the lady performer is sent flying out over the auditorium at the end of a swing held by her partner on the high trapeze. Les Andrea present a malt protean and dancing act in which Mons Andre excels as a dancer of rag-time, Ilussian and horn-pipe terpsichorean selections. His agility and lightness of foot compel admiration. Tom E. Finglass, the original cowboy coon, makes effective play with a wigwam, a wife, a baby and a silvery moon. He has a couple of good songs set to attractive music, and altogether contrives to present a pleasing. turn. Madame Celina Bobe excels both as a violinist and xylophoniste. Her ilaying of both these widely dis- similar instruments is marked by finished technique and considerable expression. She had an excellent reception and a recall. Mr. and Mrs. George Leonard are both good in the sketch "Coming home." The subject is hackneyed and the sketch rather too long but it has some witty dialogue in it and aroused plenty of laughter. Barry Nelson, come- dian, does not appear to have reached that stage of discrimination which enablas a man to distinguish between real humour and bald vulgarity. His crino- line conception is neither new nor nice. Two excel- lent pictures are shown this week and there is some excellent orchestral work particularly in the accom- paniments to Madame Bobe. RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr C E Linaker presiding.

GRAPPENHALL SEWERAGE SCHEME.

The Local Government Board wrote with reference to the application of the Rural District Council for sanction to borrow sums amounting to £18,500 for purposes of sewerage and sewage disposal for tbe con- tributing places of Grappenhall, Latchforci Without, and Thelwall, enclosing a copy of a memorial which had been addressed to the Board respecting this matter, and the Local Government Board requested that they might be furnished with the observations of the Council, especially with regard.to the statement as to an existing sewer and that as to charging against Thelwall, the cost of the works outside the parish. The Clerk submitted a lengthy reply which be bed formulated upon the memorial and on tbe points raised thereon, which was approved by the Council. It was understood, however, that in consequence of there not being a quorum, at the last meeting of the Sewage Com- mittee, the matter Would have to stand over.

MEDICAL OFFICF.R. The Medical Officer submitted his report which showed that in the case of three diphtheria patients the necessary action had been taken. One of the patients had died, the other two being the only cases in the Isolation Hospital. The report was adopted.

The Cltrk reported that the superiorees of Fantosaph Orphanage bad written stating that in view of the con- siderable increase in the cost of provisions she solicited the Board's kindly considerAtion in the way of a weekly increase to the amount now contributed by the Board towards the maintenance of children chargeable there. She sincerely trusted that the Board would kindly entertain the suggestion in the spirit in which it was offered. The persistent increase in the cost of commodities was vetting serious. The Clerk pointed out that at the present time they were contributing 4s , which was less than the amount It would take to keep the children in the Workhouse. Mr. Morris moved that the amount be increased to ss. He could bear out the superioress's testimony as to the increased cost of living. The cost of provisions and living generally had gone up considerably, and bet thought the request they had before them was a very reasonable one Mr Woodward seconded. Mr Morris understood that they were the lowest contributors to the Orphanage, and he thought they could pay the 5s per week. Mr Linaker : It is a very reasonable request. The motion was then carried by the meeting. Mr. _Rigby Swift a K.C.

The new list of K.C.'n-issued on Monday contained the name of Mr. Rigby Swift, M.P., for St. Helens who is well-known in Warrington, both as a legal advocate and a politician. He was called to the Bar at the early age of 21. If anything be has practised more in Liverpool and the western part of the county than in Manchester, but he has figured in more than one cause celebre at the Manchester Assize Courts. He is a skilful lawyer and an able advocate. Ile won a victory for Unionism in the St. Helens Division at the last General Election, when he defeated Mr. Thomas Glover. the Labour member, who had held the seat from NOG.

WHISKY STOLEN FROM R AILW sv.--Arthur Thompson railway fireman, 227. Winwick-road, was charged on remand with stealing on the 20th September two bottles of whisky from Hallam Sidings, the property of the Land N-W Railway Co.—The case as outlined by the prosecuting solicitor was that on the day in ques- tion the prisoner left his engine and returned with two bottles of whisky, which he said he had taken from a van. He took the bottles away, and later, railway detectives found the empty bottles at his lodgings. When charged he denied stealing them, but said he found them planted. Mr Browne, who defended, put in a plea of guilty, and advanced the circumstances in extenuation that the man Lind been in the railway com- pany's service for 20 years without having a complaint against him, lie had now, of course, lost his position. He got down from the engine to inquire about a stoppage on the line and found the bottles hidden. Foolishly he took them home, thinking that finding was keeping. The Chairman said Thompson had been guilty of a serious error, but the railway company must protect themselves. They would he as lenient as possible by imposing a fine of 40s including costs.
