===== 0002601_19121012_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
Christ Church, Latchford.

Sermons were preached at Christ Church, Latchford, on Sunday, on behalf of the Archbishop's Western Canada Fund by the Rev R Adams, of Indian Head, Qu'appelle. In the course of his sermons the preacher said the Archbishops of Canterbury and York,some two years and a half ago, had called attention to the critical position of the faith in Canada, into which emigrants were pouring at the rate of 350.000 a year. They had appealed to the country at large, in the name of patriotism and religion, for men and money. There was a possibility of saving whole areas of a young and virile community for the Church of their fathers if only assistance were, forthcoming from England. Unless such help was giTen the scandal of our negligence towards the States of America in the 18th century would be repeated. It was the duty and the privilege of every Englishman at the present critical time to help in saving from materialism and godlessness the thousands of his fellow-countrymen who were pouring into Canada. The collections amounted to .£9 23. 10d. The Parish Church New Vestry. A special meeting of the Parish Church Council was held at the Rectory to consider a proposal made by Mr Isherwood, the people's warden, to change the site of the new vestry from the south to the north side of the chancel. The advantages of this change were fully explained and unanimously approved. The following great advantages will be secured by the erection of the choir vestry on the north side : The choir will not be disturbed during the erection of the new building ; the present vestry will be left intact for the use of the clergy ; there will be no need to remove any existing remains beneath or near the present vestry ; there will be direct access to the new vestry from the main entrance to the churchyard ; the new building will be, we trust, an ornament to the church and will be visible to all ; hotter accommodation will be provided close to the church for vestry meetings, choir practices and various parochial purposes, and the room will be well warmed, being close to the heating apparatus. A further meeting of the Council was held at the Rectory at which a plan of the new vestry was submitted, with an estimate by the architect, Mr Seger Owen, and both were approved.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Mothers Indicted.

Bishop Thinks they Regard Children as Playthings.

Dr. Chavasse, Bishop of Liverpool, in dedicating extensions at Lowton St. Mary's Church schools on Sunday night, said :—" I sin speaking as a parent to parents. What we in England have to grieve for at present as a real national peril is the shirking of responsibility by parents—parents allowing their children to do very much what they like. In the upper classes of society we see too often fathers and mothers bent upon pleasure,and regarding their children not as loans to be brought up for Christ and to be given back to him, but as mere playthings. A child is banded over at an early age to the nurse. The mother is too busy to look after the child. Then a little later the child is handed over to the governess or the tutor, And fathers and mothers only see their children during the holidays. The object amongst the unper classes, in a good many cases, seems to be to save themselves all the trouble they can in bringing up their children. " The Bishop went on to express the belief that parents of children in villas, in artisan dwellings, and cottages, were equally to blame for the stream of iniquity in our great cities, fed by undisciplined offspring.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Liverpool Cathedral. Appeal for £65,000.

Further progress in the Liverpool Cathedral scheme was disclosed at a meeting of the Executive Committee, held on Monday, at the Church House. Sir William Forwood presided. It was reported that the chancel, the vestries, chapter house, and the ambalatory could be finished within three years, and that the committee had all the furniture and fitments promised with the exception of the bishop's throne. The chancel will accommodate a congregation of 417 without the side aisles. The committee have sufficient funds in hand to enable them to complete this portion of the work, but after that there will be only about £14,000 available towards providing adequate accommodation for the congregation. Nevertheless, the committee have decided to proceed with the building of the first of the transepts at a cost of £79,000, and thus afford further accommodation for about 1,500 persons. This particular work will be com- pleted about three years hence, so that the transept will be consecrated at the same time as the chancel. In undertaking this fresh responsibility of raising £65,000 within the next three years, the committee are confident that they can rely upon the continued liberality of Churchmen in Liverpool and district to enable them to complete this—the first portion of their great work—within three years' time. A letter was read from Mrs Barrow offering to con- tribute a further sum of £2,000 towards tho cost of the organ, making her total contributions for the organ .C 17,000.

The Autumn Absurdity. The late and disappointing ingethering (writes the Rev A M Mitchell in the Burtonwood Parish Magazine) has sorely perturbed hona-flde harvest festivellers i.e. the once a year churchgoers. The possibility of losing their pet autumn function, in ecclesiastical surround- ings. has greatly disturbed the minds of these lovers of a religious vegetable show. For these be the harvest good, be the harvest bad, "the festival" must be held. However belated the ingathering, however lean the crops, churches and chapels must be requisitioned for an exhibition deemed a quid pro quo for a year's alms- giving. We want the show" say the irreligious laity. We want the money," says the much-badgered parson Is it so? Must we have quasi-religious shows in our churches to meet our reliAious obligations? Apparently our parsons and ministers think so—but alas! their thoughts are not always heavenly I Of natural wealth there is great abundance. Of church money there should be no lack. Shortage should never mark Christian work. Yet it is shortage all along the line, begging, begging, all the days, the cry is unceasing " More more." The parson, in severity-flee per cent of our churches, is simply an ecclesiastical beggar. He lives to beg, he hasp() alternative. Beg or be ruined ! The autumn ecclesiastical-vegetable show is an opportunity for the ingathering of a few pounds—a temptation so powerful as to be almost irresistible. At any time " the harvest festival" to give the autumn function its now popular title,is an anachronism, at a lean time, such as we have had this year, it is an absurdity—it is even, from the agricultnralist's point of view, a monstrosity. The festival spirit is hardly to. be expected among men who, through no fault of their own will have to face abort commons for some time to come, if they do not come altogether to grief. Why ask men to thank God for shortage? God is not unreasonable—man may be, man is. Do we usually thank God for what we have not received, for what we are not likely to receive? Do we teach children so? The well-known Grace formula runs: "for what we are about to receive may the Lord make us truly thankful." God is .not mocked. It is soil '•!. special silver mockery pure and simple to. require of the small, the very small farmer, ablations in kind and, withal to put or ri sgboal dnedn ointofre offering. The pocketlhe m to a n p bring f the fortho is not the only sufferer. The working and middle classes have a "dear winter" before them. Are we to have a "festival " in thankfulness for dear food ? Such a function is an all round autumn absurdity.

THE REV. R. PARRY JONES.

At 6.30 p.m. a meeting was held by the Presbytery in conjunction with the Warrington Cnapel to establish the Rev P. Parry Jones (late of Millom and Dalton), to the Joint Pastorate of Warrington and Earlestown. The chairman was the Rev D D Williams, Moss Side, Manchester.

Welcome speeches were delivered by Rev D D Wilflatus' Moss Side, Manchester ; Rev J Stanley Roberts,Bolton ; Mr Edward Jones, Altrincham (chair- man Manchester monthly meeting) ; Messrs D E Roberts and Mason Powell,Heywood-street,Manchester, on behalf of the Manchester monthly meeting ; also by the Rev John Owen, Aufleld,Liverpool,Mr John Morris, J.P., Liverpool and Mr John Williams, Wigan on behalf of the Liverpool monthly meeting; and by Mr W T Williams, J.P., Warrington kin behalf of the Warrington Chapel),Nir John Smith, Dalton-in-Furness, (on behalf of the churches of Millom and Dalton.)They all spoke very highly of the qualities of the Rev Parr) Jones and said that he excelled as a preacher, pastor and shepherd and was a true man of God. One speaker very humorously said that Mr Jones had risen from the ranks to be a commissioned officer in the Welsh Prebyterian army. Rev R PARRY JONES in reply said that he was deeply touched by all the good things said of him by the brethren and only wished that they were all true. Personally he felt full of faults. He had already had a very enthusiastic welcome from the Warrington Church and he felt honoured at the warm welcome received from the Man- chester monthly meeting.

Baptist Church, Golborne-street.

The harvest festival services were held on Sunday rt this Church, which had been very tastefully decorated. The Rev. H. Ogden was the preacher. In the morning he took as his text" He that reapeth receiveth wages and gatbereth fruit unto life eternal ; that he that soweth and he that reapeth may rejoice together." The afternoon service took the form of a service of song, entitled " His other sheep," the music of which was effectively rendered by the choir. Great credit is due to Mr Barnes, the choirmaster, and Mr S Rickers, the organist. The reader was Mr J Jarman. There were good congregations at the morning and afternoon services, but the church was crowded at night. The choir rendered three anthems with taste and effect— Ye shall dwell in the land," "On mercy's errand" and "0, Lord, how manifold." In the evening the Rev II Ogden took for his text Jeremiah viii. 20 : " The harvest is past, the summer ended, and we are not saved." He said there were times when an optimist was the blind leader of the blind. No good was served by ignoring facts. Wise was the man who stored his mind and soul with good things that his old age might be a time when he gathered together all the good of his youth.

_ Primitive Methodist Church, Legh-street. On Thursday the first of a series or monthly devotional services was held. S. John's Presbyterian Church. pr,Rching at St. John's on Sunday evening last on the subject of "Jesus, the supreme teacher," Mr Reid took for his text the words "Be ye not called Rabbi, for one is your teacher and ye are all brethren." The Jews. said Mr Reid, although specially trained to receive God's revelation of truth had sunk into bond- age of thought, through allowing truth to be displaced by the fathers' traditions, so that when Christ appealed past tradition to truth they were incapable of receiving Him. The history of the Church fully justified Christ's concern that His revelation of truth should not be over- laid and blighted in the same way. The Church rested and continued to rest upon councils and con- fessions, and surrendered its birthright of liberty to appeal only to Him, the Truth. The work of the Reformation had been to restore to the people the Word of God, the heart of which was Christ in His life and teaching. Whatever was their attitude to dogma they had no right to place it as a test upon anyone. Not everyone who was orthodox enough to say" Lord !Lord!" but he that doeth the will of God. The Fatherhood of God and the brotherhood of man' both finding their highest expression in Christ, the forgetfulness of self, the clean heart and the service of love, was the creed He would see embodied in human life, affording liberty for everything except sin. That was the creed by which the primitive Church lived and did its work of evan- gelization.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe Congregational Church.

The Rev C F Richardson, 11I.A , of Wigan, occupied the pulpit morrling and evening, at this church on Sunday. In his address to the children he drew a lesson from the autumnal season, when the fruit and grain are garnered, and the withered and useless refuse burnt. lie proceeded to tell an attractive story, show- ing that according to our good deeds and works here, so shall our future elori be. His sermon was based on the text "Never man spake like this man" (John vii. 46). This evidence of the officers of the Pharisees, said the preacher, was an unsolicited testimonial to the power of Jesus. Surely they has) evidence there, if evidence were Deeded that Jesus Christ was unique and extraordinary. Not only was it necessary to confute those who denied the claims of Christ, but it was also necessary that Christ's followers should be brought face to face with His words and deeds so that they might feel His power and acknowledge that never man spake like Him. It was not so much the opponents of Christianity that the Church had to fear as the indiffer- ence, coldness and lack of response which the people within the Church exhibited when they heard the words of Christ.

- - The subject of the evening's discourse was Christ's commendation of Mary's action in anointing Him. (Mark xiv, 8-9.) He pointed out that the praise bestowed upon Mary's action was called forth because of the criticism of the onlookers. They needed to learn that very often the criticism of the world called forth the praise of Christ rather than His rebuke. There were those who said that money spent in main- taining Christian churches and organisations should be spent on schemes of social amelioration. That was the same plea urged in Mary's case. Whilst Ram true that we wanted social reform and schemes for the better- ment of the poor; as was the case in Christ's da3, let them not forget the Christ. The preacher proceeded to urge all Christian workers to renewed activity, and not to be disheartened by the criticism of the world, for very often such criticism had proved superficial.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Cairo-street Chapel. "The Inequalities of Life."

Harvest thanksgiving services were held at thia chapel on Sunday when the Rev. Dr. St•inley Mellor preached morning and evening. Mr. A. J. Mounfield conducted a children's service in the afternoon. The congregations were excellent and in the evening the chapel was filled to overflowing. The choir, under the direction of Mr. J. M. Muir, rendered special music. Dr. Mellor took for the subject of his morning dis- course" Continuity in the spiritual life" and in the evening he preached on "The inequalities of life." lie said this earth of ours was amazingly rich ; she responded to the labour of man in a manner truly marvellous, pouring forth her treasure with unstinted measure. She was really overflowing with the things that were requisite and necessary for the preservation and health of our physical organisms. Even though vast tracts of her territory lay unculti- vated, preserved for the selfish sport or seltisn pleasare of a few, still she provided enough and to spare for her millions. It was only here and there in the world that population exceeded the power of sustenance and, with a little care and foresight, that could be righted. Th is earth was really a bountiful and abundant mother ; the wealth of the world was vast beyond all conception and in the end all that incredible wealth depended on mother earth. Yet, in the midst of so much abounding plenty, there were starving men, women and children who had not the remnant of a crust of bread to put between their teeth. Go round the world and try to number those creatures; they would not et( p at hundreds, nor at thousands, nor at tens of thousands. And the cause ot their starving was not tbat there was a lack of food in the world. Was the whole earth suffering from famine that one man should go hungry ? Was starvation threatening the human race and compelling it to sacrifice children to preserve its own life ? Were we living in a state of siege, beset by foes who cut off our food supplies, that these things should he? Nor should we say that it was their own fault, that they starved because they were idle or drunken or dissolute. Some few such there might be amongst them, but, even so, when and by whom was it decreed that society should punish its weak and diseased members by starvation ; and what of the children ? Were they also drunken and idle and dissolute ? No, the unfortunates who starved in this world of plenty did so not because of their own fault, nor because the earth did not provide enough food for all, but for some totally different reason. " All good gifts around us," svid the hymn, " are sent from heaven above." That was doubtless the case, but then, surely the access of men, who were reputed to be God's children, to the gifts of God, should be free and equal. The very opposite, however, seemed to be the case. For the vast majority of mankind free and equal access to the bountiful gifts of nature seemed to be cut off by a most astonishing number of barriers for some of them, the intervening barriers were so numerous and so bard to surmount that, by the time they had managed, with difficulty, pain and toil, to get over them and reach the place where the gifts of God were stored, they found there was remarkably little left to be divided. When they got there the cupboard was bare. The first barrier to which the preacher called attention was that of work—in itself a fine and noble thing, a thing to be honoured and loved, but for some reason or other, turned by the evil machinations of men, into an agonizing drudgery, a degrading bondage, an undignified slavery. Consequent on that, there were also the barriers of low wages, uncon- genial employment, long hours spent in servitude. constant anxiety for the future, and many others, all of which would have to be swept clean away before the divine justice of God who provided sustenance for His creatures was reflected in the activities of men who distributed the sustenance amongst themselves. So evil were those man-made barriers, that he who turned the soil and sowed the seed, hardening his hands and bending his back, given no peace and leisure, received at last the scantiest share of all in the ultimate harvest, in the shape of a life that was ignorant, brutish and mean and a measure of subsistence which was barely enough to preserve the fading life in his body. All the glorious and profuse gifts of nature were being turned to base uses, set to serve mammon instead of God, offered as sacrifices upon the altar of gain, instead of on the altar of good—and that because there was one false idea suffered to dwell in the midst

A CONTRADICTION IN TERMS.

I could the better have appreciated the humour of an item in the repertoire of the excellent artiste who entertained a Lymm gatherit g a few nights ago if, in representing an excited spectator at a football match, he had made him an Irishman, or even a Lancashire or a Yolkshire man. Who ever came across "an excited Scotchman" in real life? The Scot is canny, he is alert, pushful, cool, calculating,and always keeps an eye on the main chance, and he is notoriously clannish, and ever ready to look not only on his own things but also oa the things of his " brither Scot," but excited to the point of oblivion to his suiroundings —never ! As to his clqrninant characteristic— his extreme care, first for his personal advantage, aid then for ti e interests of fellow- country men, I may add an illugration. A Corpora- tion whose proceedings I used to report years ago embarked on a new enterprise, but it certainly was not a success until after two or threa years costly experience, they imported a nrnager from Scotland. Then matters began to go more smoothly and satisfac- torily, and gradually tl e concern was made to pay its way. So far all interested in the works had nothing but praise for the efficient Scotsman. After a while, however, foremen and others found things gradualy ' becoming It ss c mfentable, and eventually places rapidly fell vacant. Everyone of these was promptly filled by a Scotsman from the very di•trict whence the manager bad crime, and presently five of the best 1-erths were so occupied.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 12, 1912.

THE MUNICIPALISATION OF HOSPITALS.

Special to the "Examiner."

The most urgent social need of the present time is the reorganisation of the medical pro- fession, and the adjustment of its relations to the general public. The National Insur- ance Act has done much to bring matters to a crisis, but it would be a gross mistake to suppose that that much misrepresented measure has been the solo cause of the pre- sent discontent and unrest in the ranks of thO medical profession. There have been two great underlying causes and the chief of these has been the immense scientific advance in the knowledge of the nature of disease and the processes of life during the past twenty years. So great has been this advance that few even in the ranks of the , profession-have been fully able to appreciate it. This has led to the apotheosis of pre- ventive medicine with it various powers of dealing with infectious disease, with the training and registration of midwives an c' other hygienic measures all of which tend to diminish the work and the income of the average general practitioner. The other great underlying cause has been the economic changes of the same period, especially that form of minor socialism known as the joint stock enter- prise. Instead of following the spirit of the times in economic matters, the medical pro- fession has been compelled to revert to the crudest individualism with its inevitable soul killing competition. it is due to this that we have with us that form of cheap club practice so justly condemned by the recent Poor-Law Commission. _ _ _

Up to about thirty years ago a qualified practitioner could by engaging unqualified assistants carry on a large practice, derive a good income, and have a fair amount of leisure to attend to other duties. The assist- ants carried on the routine of practice while the principal attended to special cases, to hospital and other public duties. This system largely aided the progressive develop- ment of the voluntary hospital system and many of the choicest fruits of medical science were nurtured and garnered by prominent men associated with the voluntary hospitals. I The working of the Medical Act of 1858 and its subsequent amendments and additions gradually caused an abolition of the unqualified assistant until now they are as a body quite extinct and only fully qualified men exist. This has led to a great increase,l of individual practices and a corresponding diminution of assistants, and this in time has led to fierce and fiercer competition among qualified men,with disastrous results both to the public and the profession. While the profession has retrogressed in an economic sense the voluntary hospital system established under the old regime has developed in accordance with the spirit of the age along joint stock lines, and the same men who direct large commercial enterprises also direct—largely to their credit be it said —the voluntary hospital system. The medical profession as a body have no voice in the matter. The National Insurance Act has brought the problem of the voluntary hospitals well to the front, and the other day at the con ference of the British Hospitals Association, the Lord Mayor of Birmingham told his audience, that sooner or later either the state or the municipalities would take over the hospitals. He preferred the. latter course. The task of taking over the voluntary hospitals is not so great as might be imagined. Already the State and the municipalities own and manage Poor Law and Fever Hospitals whose beds far out- number those of the voluntary hospitals, and if we view the lunatic asylums as mental hospitals the number of beds controlled now by the State and the municipalities is double those controlled by the voluntary hospital authorities. It is not generally recognised that there is no need for special legislation to deal with this important question. The Public Health Act passed by a Tory Government in 1875 provides for this under Section 131. There it is given that any local authority may build or acquire hospitals for the sick, and sickness has been held to include all forms of sickness whether infectious or not—even accidents. The hospitals-at Widnes and Barry may be cited as examples. It is generally stated that the operation of the National Insurance Act will tend to cripple the voluntary hospitals by the with- drawal of subscriptions. Dr. Basil Rhodes, the secretary of the North Staffordshire Infirmary, is not so pessimistic; he even believes that by suitable measures the sub- scriptions may be increased. Whether he be right or not the hospitals must be continued, extended and organised in accordance with the progress of scientific medicine • that is largely in the direction of specialisation. The working of the Insur- ance Act in Germany proved the inadequacy of the voluntary hospitals there and the Insurance Authorities were compelled to build their own hospitals. The staffs of these hospitals are paid and the system runs smoothly without frantic appeals to the public for funds. In the near future the British public must make up its mind as to how its hospitals are to be sustained, extended and organised. Probably the course taken will be that of municipalisation with some degree of central control by the Local Government Board.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY.

ITS 250th ANNIVERSARY.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Dr. Charles Owen and the

The early Nonconformist meeting houses were designed so closely upon one pattern that it is safe to say that the first Warring- ton chapel followed in every important feature the buildings described in our last chapter. It stood upon the site, or part of the site, of the present building, which succeeded it in the middle of the eighteenth century. We are left without any certain knowledge of its form or distinctive features, and may perhaps assume that it had little architectural distinction or merit. That it was removed to give place to a second chapel after about forty years suggests that it was smaller than its successor. On the other hand, some statistics which we shall have to quote later go to show that a large build- would be needed to contain any considerable part of the congregation which gathered under Dr. Owen's ministry. Dr. Halley, whose acquaintance with dissenting chapels began a century ago, and who was in touch with a generation earlier still seems to suggest that the first chapel

was the pattern upon which the present chapel was built and that it does not differ very considerably from it. He wrote: " Alth,ugh the original meeting house in Warrington was taken down in 1715 and the present building :erected in its place, the goo 1 reople who designed the latter seemed to have adhered so scrupulously to the true Presbytertan style of architecture as to have furnished a perfect representative of tie early sanctuary of their fathers. It aprears more venerable, more puri- tanic, more genuine and uncorrupted in its dtcay than any other of the Lancashire meeting-houses erected in the middle of the eighteenth centmy," It is, of course, very possible that the present building is an extension of the older one and that some of the original walls are undisturbed, but to our great misfortune no account of the erection of either building has been preserved.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
First Warrington Academy.

The clergyman whose violence gave him leadership in this miserable clamour—though perhaps it would be more correct to describe him as the tool rather than the leader of the High Church Party—was Henry Sacheverell. Samples of his preaching bring the blush to good men of all parties and we make quota- tions with diffidence. The Academies set up at great sacrifice by the Dissenters were denounc,•cd a "fountains of lewdness from which were spawned all descriptions of heterodox, lewd and atheistical books."

He was'supported in his attack by Samuel Wesley, the father of John and Charles, who sent forth from his quiet rectory "A Letter from a country Divine," in which he almost equalled Sacheverell in the strength of his abuse. THE AUTHOR OF "ROBINSON CRUSOE."

Among those who replied on the Dissent- ing side was Daniel Defoe famous as the author of "Robinson Crusoe." The attack of Sacheverell drew from him a tract which has become celebrated in the annals of

controversy—his "Shortest way with the Dissert?rs." The tract was written in the approved Sacheverell manner. It suggested that, Nonconformist ministers should be hariged and that the rank and file should be banished from the land.

It was a "short and easy way with the Dissenters" indeel, which this delightful writer described.

Of course it was extraordinary and far- fetched satire. By a piece of extravagance Defoe meant to show up Sacheverell and set the country in laughter against him. The curious thing is that both sides failed to see through it. The Dissenters took it seriously and regarded Defoe as a traitor. The High Church party found the tract none too strong for them. Hanging and banishing was exactly to their taste, and they belauded the author not only as a splendid convert but as a prophet. When poor Defoe explained that it was a huge hoax their .fury was terrible. He was fined, imprisoned and had his ears hacked off in the pillory. The book suffered the usual fate of heretical literature, in being publicly burned by the hangman, but not before it had been read and discussed the country through. The first blow at Nonconformity failed. It I took the, form of a Bill for the repeal of the Occa,si,onal Conformity Act which meant in effect that Dissenters should be excluded from every civil, military or naval office and kept, entirely out of public life. This was passed by the Commons but rejected by the Lords.

But the tide was running strongly against the Dissenters and the rejection of the Bill was received with very widespread anger. The excitement, too, ran through society and was delightfully satirized by Dean Swift, who after describing it as the highest and warmest reign of-party feeling he ever knew, wrote to Stella "It was so universal that I observed the dogs in the streets were much more contumelious and quarrelsome than usual, and the very night the Bill went up, a committee of Whig and Tory cats had a very warm and loud debate upon the roof of our house. But why should we wonder at that, when the very ladies are split asunder into High Church and Low, and out of zeal for religion have hardly time to say their prayers." Failure increased the anger of the High Church party and Sacheverell renewed his campaign in an extraordinary sermon preached before the Lord Mayor of London. The Nonconformists were denounced as "monsters and vipers," "Miscreants begat in rebellion, born in sedition and nursed in fiction." They were " filthy dreamers" despisers of the Crown, and guilty of treason Under the title "Perils from false Brethren " this toothsome utterance was printed and circulated in tens of thousands by admiring High. Churchmen. _ _ _

But the preacher had gone too far for the Government. He had poured contempt on the first minister of the Crown and was tried and sentenced to deprivation for three years. This, however, increased rather than diminished his popularity and frenzied crowds throughout the country took up his battle cries.

Parliament under the spell of alarm passed a Bill not only against Occasional Conformity but a much more serious measure under the title "A Bill to prevent the growth of Schism and for the further security of Church of England as by Law established." THE SCHISM ACT. This is familiarly known as the "Schism Bill" and enacted that no person should teach the young either in school or college or privately without conforming to the Church of England and obtaining a license from a Bishop.

- This made, of course, a speedy end of Dissenting Academies and left the Noncon-

THE OLD CHAPEL AT KNUTSFORD, ERECTED 1683.

A good example of the early chapels described in the last article. There are two outer stairways which give access to the gallery. Mrs. Gaskell the well-known novelist attended the chapel in her early years and described it in "Ruth." Her grave is in the background of the picture.

formists without means to educate either their own children or their own ministers.

Amonathe Academies that were closed under this fresh persecution was one in War- rington which had Dr. Owen as its presiding genius, and his house, in all probability as its home.Of this early Academy we would gladly learn more. A few references which give only the veriest glimpses of the school have come under our notice and that is all.

One is in a description of Jonathan Woodworth : " The authour of these letters was a sober, godly, hopgul youth a good schollcr ad one yt apply'd himself but too close to his studys. He devoted himself to ye ministiy, and was some- time under ye direction and Tutorage of ye Reverend Mr. Charles Owen of Warrington, till ye Seism Bill took place, and then Mr. Owen , desisted and this young man went to Glasgow. . . The Academy was probably small. It is doubtful whether theology was taught and it does not appear to have received financial help from Presbyterian funds. 'But it helped in the making of ministers and was grate- fully remembered by more than one who passed from its portals to find influence and fame.

Among the scholars who spoke kindly of it was Job Orton the close friend and assist- ant of Dr. Doddridge and who was invited to become the successor to that truly noble man.

It may have been the service rendered in this unpretentious way, joined perhaps with an appreciation of his literary work, that brought Dr. Owen under the regard of the Edinburgh University.ln 1728 he received from that University the degree of Doctor of Divinity and had as his companion in honours the famous singer to whom Nonconformity was yet to owe so much, Dr. Isaac Watts. It speaks something for the influence and learning of the Nonconformist ministers that drastic legislation was pushed through Parliament to destroy their schools. Once the day of repression was over they rapidly opened again, and Dr. Owen did good service by his tutorage. But there were to be many stormy days before then. His three years of enforced silence over, Sacheverell passed through the country in a triumphal procession with the cry " The Church is in danger," and excited mobs answered "Sacheverell for ever. "Down with the Whigs and Dissenters." " God save King James 111. " He did not visit Lancashire, but the frenzy was universal and was soon to bear bitter fruit. QUEEN ANNE'S " FUNERAL " SERMONS.

In the midst of the tumult Queen Anne died. Small wonder that the Nonconformists gave way to rejoicing, and that their funeral sermons were of unusual flavour. Dr. Owen celebrated the event by preaching from I. Kings 16-30, "Ahab the son of Omri did evil in the sight of the Lord above all that were before him," and if his sermon had the severity of some of his writings the congrega- tion had strong food on that happy morning. But he was quite eclipsed by his friend Thomas Bradbury in the matter of finding a really appropriate text. This able divine kept the "mournful" occasion by preaching a funeral sermon from 11. Kings, 9-34 "Go see now this cursed woman, and bury her, for she is a king's daughter." It is worth mentioning that Bradbury was able to make the first public announcement of the death of the Queen and the accession of the present House of Hanover. Thomas Bradbury was miniSter of Fetter-lane Church in London, and had a message sent to him from the Queen's bedside by Bishop Burnet. A handkerchief dropped from the gallery was the signal. He closed his sermon and the wondering congregation heard him pray for "George, King of Great Britain and Ireland."

===== 0002601_19121012_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. CHAR3,ES OWEN.

The preacher who had the satisfaction of ministering in Warrington's first chapel, and whose influence gave Nonconformity a firm place in the life of the town was Dr. Charles Owen. This learned and zealous pastor suc- ceeded Peter Aspinwall in 1696, and after about seven years of ministry gathered his flock within the walls of the new meeting- house. The half-century of Dr. Owen's ministry in Warrington (from 1696 to 1746) witnessed many changes in Church and State and brought many anxious days for Noncon- formists. Some of these events and some of his services to the cause during chequered and memorable years we must begin to briefly tell in the present chapter. Charles Owen was born and had his early home in Bryn near Carmarthen. His father and mother were devoted members of the Church, yet all their nine children became zealous Nonconformists. Three sons, David, James and Charles, of whom Charles was the youngest, became ministers, the two former doing good service to Nonconformity in Wales. Charles Owen came to Warrington in 1696 seven years after the passing of the Act of Toleration. It was a period of decided Nonconformist activity, for tho Act of Toleration while it left Dissenters exposed to many civil disadvantages and though it left the Test Act and Corporation Act unrepealed, gave them a fixed and sheltered place in the life of the nation, and they at once began to attract thoughtful and depend- able people of the business and artizan classes. But in high places the current turned strongly against them. In the State, Toryism gained asendency. In the Church what we should now call the High Church party became the real power. The Bishops were Whig in politics and favourable to the dissenters, but the bulk of the clergy were Tory and Jacobite. Such were opposed to the policy of toleration ; they made, moreover, no secret of their enmity to William and when he died in 1702 they exulted with an unholy gladness.

QUEEN ANNE. The coming of Queen Anne darkened, what was already, for Nonconformists, a clouded sky. The Queen .avowed herself a High Churchwoman and let the Dissenters feel the strength of her hatred. When they presented a loyal address she received them in discourteous silence, and in her first address from the Throne said in plain words that she was determined to countenance those who were zealous to support the Church of England. There was a speedy riddance of the Whig ministers of William, followed by the instal- ment of Marlborough and Godolphin. Nonconformity was so entirely identified with the Whig party that its leaders braced themselves for cheerless days. The storm quickly gathered. The High Church party saw their advantage, and began a bitter agitation, The first object of attack being the Dissenting Academies.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
RESURRECTION.

Yet another story of Thomas Bradbury. On the accession of George I. in 1714, the Nonconformist ministers presented a loyal address. They wore Geneva gowns and as they passed towards the King a nobleman whispered sarcastically to Bradbury "Is this a funeral ? "

"No my lord" he said "It is a Resurrection."

A resurrection it was ; but some trying days had yet to be faced, for the year follow- ing brought the Jacobite Rebellion with its accompaniment of mob-rule and the wrecking of Dissenting Chapels.

A TRIP TO THE MOON. How would you like to take a trip to the moon? It would be a long journey, and would require- much preparation, but no doubt the results would pay us. Let us see what would be required. In the first place we should require some means of transit. If we went by an aerial express train it would take us six months, and even if we could arrange to be shot out of a modern cannon it would take five or six days. Our outfit would have to be very extensive—much more so than for a voyage to the North Pole. Indeed, we should have to take everything with us. In addition to clothing we should need both food and drink, and the problem cf keeping it from freezing would not be an easy one to solve. There is practically no air on the moon, and so we should require a supply for breathing; and if, in addition, we needed a fire to thaw our water, should require an extra quantity to keep the fire going. Supposing that these difficulties could be overcome, and that we were safely landed upon the moon's surface, we should begin to experience a long series of surprises. What a scene of desolation all around The desert of Sahara would look like a garden in com- parison with a lunar landscape. Not a blade of grass, not a tree, or brook,or lake—nothing but a vast silent desert, uninhabited by bird, or beast, or insect. In the distance we should see rugged mountains, steep and forbidding. Our mountains on earth are smoothed by the influence of rain and frost, but these lunar mountains are as rugged as though left by an earthquake. As a matter of fact they are huge, extinct volcanoes, still clothed in the lava that they emitted long ages ago. One of the first things that would surprise us would be the strange appearance of the landscape. We should be able to see incredible distances, and the mountains which seemed a mile away might prove to be a hundred. We should marvel at the brilliance of the sunshine, and the intense blackness of the shadows. It would surprise us to notice that everything in the shade was in total darkness. As we should probably have a scientist in our party, he would explain to us that this is due to the fact that there is no air filled with little dust particles to scatter the sunshine and reflect it into shady corners. The sun would look pretty much as he does now, but we should be surprised to find him apparently standing still. If we arrived in the early part of the moon's day, we should I have to wait about 340 hours for sunset, that lis to say that the old world would have had 15 sunsets before we had one. Once the sun hal sunk behind the horizon our most delightful surprise would come. We should see the glorious spectacle of earth-rise. The big world bathed in wonder- ful sunshine would rise above the horizon, and we should see the continents of America, and then of Europe, Asia and Africa, glowing in wondrous light with the glittering oceans between. By the pale and beautiful light of this great orb we should be able to sit and read, or what would be much more interest- ing, watch the great world slowly revolving in the brilliant sun-light. The long night of 340 hours would give ample time for a study of the earth, and we should welcome the glare which told us that a lunar day had begun. By the aid of a railway laid around the moon we could avoid the night altogether, for it would not be hard to give chase to the sun and enjoy perpetual day. But if our locomotive broke down on the other side of the moon it would go badly with us,for there would be n 3 friendly earth to light us, and we should be left in inky darkness through a long lunar night. It is pleasant to sketch out excursions around the moon, and conjure up the picture of what our world would look like from a new standpoint, but our dreams, alas I must go unfulfilled, for the moon is an inhospitable little world, and would quickly freeze to death any adventurer who landed there.

KEEP A-TRYING. Say "I will !" and then stick to it That's the only way to do it. Don't build up awhile, and then Tear the whole thing down again. Fix the goal you wish to gain, Then go at it heart and brain ; And, though clouds shut out the blun, Do not dim your purpose true With your sighing. Stand erect, and, like a man, Know, "They who think they can" Keep a-trying. Rad Columbus, half-seas o'er, Turned back to his native shore, Man would not, to-day, proclaim Round the world his deathless name. So, we must sail on with him, Past horizons far and dim, Till at last we own the prize That belongs to him who tiros With faith und3ing ; Own the prize that all may win Who, with hope, through thick and thin Keep a-trying. THOUGHTFULNESS. The driver of a jaunting-car evidently expected a tip, for when the crabbed old gentleman handed him a sixpence, the exact legal fare for the distance driven, Paddy's face began to darken. But he would descend to no vulgar abuse of his niggardly patron. ' Instead he got down from his seat, walked to his horse's head, and threw a cloth over it. "Now, what did you do that for ?" inquired the old gentleman. " Yer honour," said Paddy," I would not have the poor beast see he had gone all that way for sixpence."

PROFITS ON ONTARIO FRUIT FARMS. The end of the harvest always brings to attention cases where exceptional profits have been made on Ontario fruit farms. The latest advice at the Ontario Government Office shows many instances where quite exceptional profits have been made. While it Is not advisable for new settlers to contemplate isolated cases where big profits have been made, it is interesting to note the average returns from crops of the fruit die tricts of the province. Grapes show a profit, after all expenses have been paid, of from £ll to £l7 per acre ,• strawberries up to £35 : peaches £55 ; pears £2O ; and currants £l2.

RECHABITE MAJORITY.

CONFERENCE AT WARRINGTON.

The Effect of the Insurance Act.

The successful celebrations of the cmaing-of-age of the Warrington Juvenile District of the Independent Order of Rechabites wore brought to a close on Saturday. In the afternoon a conference of superin- tendents and workers was htld in the Bold-street Wesleyan schoolrocm when the chief subject of dis- cussion was the position of the juvenile section of the Order in view of the operations of the insurance Act. Bro J It Barker, D.S J.T , preaided and was supported by the following officers of the juvenile section : Bros It Bell, ILSJ.T., John Dimond, P.H S J.T., Thomas Massey, J.D.S , William Baxendale, J.D T, II Pendleton, A.D.S.J T„ P Howard, Levite,l4l Briecce, Guardian, ,T Horne, J Lawton, and David Sutton ' (trustees). The adult officers in attendance were:— Bros J It Barker, D C R., D Hamblen, DDK,S , Fernley, Levite, T Winstanley, D Sec, T Massey, treasurer, J Hill, assistant secretary, E Walkden and Wright (tr ustees), J Gee, P.D.C.R. The CHAIRMAN, in the course of an address of welcome' I leaded for greater zeal and determination in the work of the Order. The drunkenness of our land was worse than famine, pestilence or war. It was the greatest enemy we bad to fear. The Rechabites boasted that they were out to make a sober nation. Let that boast not be in vain. (Applause). Bro. BaxESDALE, one of the founders of the Warring- ton Juvenile District,eaid they Lttle thought, when they started the work twenty-one years ago, that it would make such splendid progress as it had done. Of the six members who arranged for the formation of the District one (Bro John Tunstall) ptesed away only a fortnight ago. The others—Bros T Massey, W Baxendale, W Wright, W Hulme and II Warburton—still survived and three of them were actively engaged in the work. Bro WRIGHT said the success of the past should be an inspiration to higher and nobler work. They were determined to go on from conquest to conquest until Warrington was no longer noted for the traffics which it was the object of the order to put down. (Applause.) Bro MassaY also joined in the welcome. Bro JOHN DIMOND, who is eighty-four years of age, was accorded a great reception on rising to acknowledge the welcome. Ile is still remarkably hale end hearty and his speech was full of good humour yet instinct with determination and earnestness. Ile said he had closely watched the work of the Warrington District and had been delighted with the progress it had made. The boys and girls were the very essence of the Order. Bro Dimond then gave reminiscences of the inception of the juvenile work, the rough and ready methods which were originally adopted in training the children, and said thy, with the juvenile section the success of the Order and, to a large extent, of the temperance movement as a whole, was bound up. Referring to the remarkable headway which the Order had made, he said that when he joined It there were only 17,000 members, all over the world, with £27,000 funds. To-day the membership must be nrarly 1,000,000 and they had £2,750,000 funds. The introduction of the Insurance Act made them feel a bit uneasy, but they were now filling up the Order with some of the best men in the country. The Order load grown until there was nothing like it under the son. (Applause). Bro. It. Behr., the High Superintendent, then gave a thoughtful and interesting address on " Juvenile work in general and its relation to the National Health Insurance Act." Ile said that wh n the Act got into full swing they would practically be placed in the posi- tion that the gaps in their ranks' caused by death rnd removals, could only he filled by transfers from the juvenile section. It was therefore wise to face the situation at once, and to make their preparations for the entirely new situation upon which the friendly societies were entering. Bro. Bell pointed out that a large proportion of the loaned members were heads of families and the accessions ta the Order's numbers, in consequence of the Act, would provide a large and fruitful field which would produce a good crop of sound and healthy lives end replenish the ravages of time. The Order must secure the children practically from the day of birth. Proscrastination was fatal, as there would be a host of canvassers on the look-out for them and the Order should endeavour to be first in the field. The attention concentrated upon , the children under the new regie.e would he tremendous I and it must be part of their sphere to see that the best interests of the children were served and not sacrificed, for the sake of agents' commissions, to swell the numbers of any society a hid] would not be careful of their moral welfare. (Rear, hear.) It would he purely a quest'cn of business with some societies to get the children. It could never be such a question with the Rechabltes, if they had the true love of the child at heart. It was not sufficient merely to receive the contribution at their tent meet- ings ; they must teach the truths of temperance, and he believed that was done in the majority of tents. The time had come when the tents should appoint visitors to the homes, to explain the principles of the Order to parents and to collect the contributions of those children who were too young to attend the meetings. Bro. Bell emphasised the useful part which ladies— notably those who belonged to the British Women's Temperance Assooiation—could play in the work of visitation. lie I roceedeel to advocate a system of grad- ing in the Order according to age, and emphasised the Importance of concentrating special attention upon the children at the critical age of between twelve and sixteen years. The Rechabites had built a bridge between the juvenile and adult sections—a bridge which few societies or churches possessed. There was a great gap between the Sunday school and the Church—a gap in which thousands of young people were lost. The Rechabites were proud of the fact that they were transferring over that bridge no fewer than 7,000 boys and girls every year. (Applause.) During the last ten years they must have transferred over 150,000 juniors into the adult ranks and they knew from experience that they made the best officers and workers. Bro. Bell advocated the introduc- tion of freshness and variety into the juvenile tent meetings and said they ought to have a juvenile choir in every district. They must draw out the beat and the highest in the child. It was not necessary to be always teaching the children the horrors and terrors of drink. They could give temperance lessons in an interesting way in connection with other subjects and get the truth home, without ostentation. The training of:superintendents was en Increasingly important feature and he hoped that a summer school will be established for their benefit. Juvenile work had not received the recognition it des- erved in some parts of the country—in fact he had some times been amazed at the indifference which prevailed. As Rechabites they bad now a golden opportunity which might never ocour again. Bro. Bell pleaded for greater co-oporation with the Band of Hope. He also mentioned that the Board had under con- sideration a form of endowment insurance, as an alternative to death benefit', With regard to medical benefits and the demands of the doctors, the fight was now going to be transferred to the arena of the local insurance committees. The Commissioners had laid down certain propositions for their guidancekand ,

EVENING MEETING.

The Evil Undermining the Nation.

Sister Mrs Dodds of Penketh p-esided over a large attendance at the evening meeting held in the Bold- street schoolrooms at which the principal speaker was Bro. It Bell, 11.5.J.T. In an interesting, inspiring and instructive speech Mrs Dodds said the object for which they were assembled made for all that was high- est and best, for the uplifting of women and men and through them 'the uplifting of" these who would constitute the nation of the future. (Hear, hear ) The Juvenile Tents Lad attained their majority end it was a most memorable time in their history. When many organizations came so quickly and vanished away so suddenly—one felt glad to knoe that the Juvenile Itechabites had attained their majority. (Hear, Lear.) They were deserving of ' every good wish, and they all hoped and trusted that they would grow, not only in wisdom but in numbers, so that the Order shonld become a real and strong power in Warrington. They knew how much it was needed and how bard the be ttle was to fight. Now-o- days it was considered quite out of fashion to he enthusiastic about anythiug. Some of the younger generation wete almost too bored to lived. She hoped that the Juvenile Itechaeites would throw into their work that wonderful enthusiasm eht.ch came from knowledge, and that they w mid do wonders. (Applause) Bro. BR.LL who was given a hearty receptioneaid that Mrs Dodds' interest in the great temperance cause was a great example to them all and it plainly showed that they could not do without women helpers in their great and useful work. (Hear, Lear.) They could all he included and by pulling together would fight the foe bravely with the knowladge that they were on the side of right. They all had their separate spheres of action and their work and duty to do in the world, but they must work unitedly, heart to heart and shoulder to shoulder, and when they had women such as were included in the British Women's Temperance Associa- tion, with them they felt strengthened to go fore ard with their good work. One if the saddest facts in the history of our country was that thinking customs amongst women were on the 'wrens°. (Ilear,hear.) They wanted to save the mothers e..ed children. They ea around them every day the havoc which drink was causing. They saw those who had gone down before the onslaught of the terrible evil and they.as Bechabites, had just cause to be thankful that they were standing erect and for all that was right. (Hear, hear.) They wanted to show the people by example Ilett it was far nobler and higher to live a pare life. All classes were uniting in that great cause. Mu who had done a hard day's work in the iron fouedry or elsewhere, gave up their Mghts to do £Olll6 little towards the amelioration of the suffering masses. What a world of meaning there was in the expression. "Bo a man and act like a man." They had hard work to do in the saving of lives and it did not matter how deeply entrenched was the wrong and how hard and how long the battle was, they were going to toil on. (Applause )When they had fought and won and laid their weapons aside, they would have the satisfaction of knowing that they bad done their share in the great work. Man was separate in a great measure from the animal kingdom end one could sue from his outward appearance that h 3 was designed for some noble work. Ile was called to high duty and to do groat tasks, to eo a great work in order to make the world better, purer and brighter. If they alwa3s kept that ideal before them, they would be inspired to do greater things and win greater victories than they had done. They were glad that the temperance movement eaa pro- gressing to steadily and achieving such good rtsults. They were driven tack in many battles, but they were still going on and by-and-by they would hoist the flag, which would be the signal of victory, on the highest summit in the enemy's country. The drink traffic was a shame and disgrace to the English-speaking race. The foreigners were comparatively free from the evil. Was it not a shame and a disgrace that we should have so many children suffering through the drink traffic ? They B9W the children in the slums, uncared for and unkempt, and they were going to help them so that they should be saved from drink and its evil influences. They would never be able to save the children until they had banished the drink curse from the land, for it was responsible for a great amount of the misery which came to those poor children. (Hear, hear). They had 250,000 boys and girls in the juvenile tents. That was something accomplished but they were out to do more. They wanted to get more and more children in their tents and tell them the truth so that they could see for themselves what was the great evil which undermined the nation. They knew of the cruel tide of intemperance and strong drink which was surging about the children and they should thank God that they had their juvenile tents to protect them and bring them safe to the shore of temperance and pure living. A nation which forgot its children was doomed to utter ruin. Thew knew from their investigations that because of the drinking habits of the people, children were cursed from their birth. " The fathers have eaten sour grapes and the teeth of the children are set on edge." They were going forward with their work for, as Mr. Chamberlain said, they dare not cease their agitation because if they did so the stones would CC y out against them. (Applause). If they got the great drink question settled once and for all they would be well on the way to solving the great social problems upon which so much money, thought and time had been spent. (Hear, hear). Mrs DODDS said she was sure they had profited very much from Mr Bell's address and that they had been greatly inspired by H. One realised from Lis remarks the dignity of the whole movement. Ile had lifted it up to the highest platform and they hoped he would be with thorn again before very long. (Applause). A capital musical programme was submitted by the • Sylvan Quartette Party (Madame Sara Siloock, Madame B Eckersley, Mr WII Dutton and Mr J &platen), who ' acquitted themselves with credit in individual and collective items. ' At the close a hearty vote of thanks was accorded all ' those who had taken part in the meeting.

MAGDALTIN COLLEGE.

The Prince of Wales will arrive at Oxford on Friday, arid, with his tutor, Mr. Rangell, will take up residence at Magdalen College in the rooms on the north of the Cloister' quadrangle which have been specially pre- pared for his occupation. The studies to which his Royal Highness will

especially devote his attention are English Literature, French and German, history, geography, and political economy. So far as possible the Prince will lead the ordinary Oxford life. Our photograph shows the quadraagle where the Prince has his' rooms, and the inset, Dr. T. Herbert Warren; President of the College.

THE INFIRMARY CONCERT.

The concert season in Warrington opened on Satur- day evening with the annual Effort of the Working- men's Committee in aid cf our premier local charity. It was pleasing to see the Parr Ball so well filed and those who attended felt well repaid by a programme of more than average merit. As usual, Mr T J Dovivr?'a orchestra, under Mr Arthur Down's conductorship, provided half a-dozen items cf a nice'y-varied character. Considering the resourccs of the bard, the quality of tone produced was somewhat disappointing; it lacked brightness and the different sections of, instrumentalists were not always perfectly in tune. There was also room for greater con rasts of expression in most of the selections and for more delicate and graceful treatment of Edward German's "Nell Gvvynne" suite. Suppe's " Pique dame" overture was given in spirited and, on the e hole, finished style and proved very popular with the audience. The committee is indebted to Mr. Down's orchestra for willing services extending over many yeats and their public-spiritedness in thus annually assisting a splendid cause is beyond praise. For vocal talent the promoters had selected artistes who have close associations eith Warrington and tars fact considerably enhanced the interest of the soncert. Miss Marie Houghton was the soprano and her cul- tured singing charmed everyone. Her beautifully sweet voice was used in truly aitistie yet natural sty'e, and while she invested her intepretations*of " Mifanwy " (Forster) and "Annie Laurie " (given in response loan encore)with much feeling, there was not the slightest tendency to exaggeration. " Who is Sylvia ' (Schubert) and "The Wood Pigeon " (Lehmann) were sung to ith delightful taste and piquancy, and the moods of rapture and despair in " l'hyllis has such charming graces " (Lane IVilson) were well realized. Mr J Pabbitt sang "The Trumpeter " (Dix) and " Dear Heart " (Toeti) with characteristic soulfulness. It is always a pleasure to listen to Mr Rabbit', but he has seldom been heard to better advantage than en this occasion. He has the gift of abandoning hineseH so com- pletely to the spirit of evelything he sings that he never fails to make an impression. He was twice encored and acknowledged the plaudits with "Two Eyes of Grey " and " Myra." Mr H. Nurse possesses a resonant bass voice but he apparently phiced too severe a strain upon it in the Opening verse of " Neptune " (Gurnee) with the result that it proved unequal to the closing passages. Ur. Nurse, however, made ample amends with an adequate renaering of " The Watchman "(Squire), for which he was recalled, responding with '• Go to sea, young man " a song much to the taste of the audience. The Stella Quartet (Messrs. A. Grounds, J. W. Willett, C. Hale and W. W. Whittle) were responsible for a novelty, in the form of humorous quartettes, the only ground for criticism being the tendency to emphasise the burlesque element at the expense of the vocal side. There was some capital fooling in " Maenamara's Band" (Etamford) and " Mrs. Cosy's Boarding House" (May), but, from the musical pout of view. " A catastrophe " (Sprague) was more pleasing. Mr J P Stafford rcunded off the programme with some capital humorous sketches, his description of an evening party, although on some- what familiar lines, having distinctly original touches which greatly amused the audience, while he made the most of all his songs and jokes. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., played the pianoforte accompaniments with his usual taste and judgment.

DISESTABLISIIMENP. A new handbook on the case for the Disestablish ment and Disendownlent of the Church of Englaud in Wales has just been issued by the Central Campaign Committee, 134, St. Ltephen's House, London, S.W. The volume contains a well-written history of the relation between Church and State .n Wales, from the earliest times to the present age, end relates how the inhabitants of that couLtry have emphasized their political demands during the Fast thirty years, for a generous consideration of their grievance from the hands of successive Parliaments. To the student of the subject the chapters dealing with the history of tithes and the rights of the poor to a portion of the endowments, shculd prove of great value at this period. A copy of the present Government measure is appended,together with exhaustive explanatory notes on Its several sections. The volume is issued in paper covers at Gd., or in cloth at is.

Mr Francomb said that on June 3rd, 1903 the com- mittee wrete the following letter to the Lunacy Com- rnissioners " rho question of fencing the clay pit in the immediate neighbourhood of the a 53 turn in order to prevent any accidents to patients is being considered. It has been suggested that a fencing of barbed wire seven feet high should be put round. I shall be glad to know IA hether your Board would have any objection to such a form of protection." The Commissicners replied that there was no objection to the fence, which was accordingly provided. If the jury mu& any recom- mendation it would receive immediate attention.

The Coroner : Our view is that the fence should be made absolutely unclimbable. Is the pond any use? Mr Erancomb : It is gradually being filled up, sir. The Coroner : Of course, we are not attaching any blame to anybody, because it is always easy to be wise after the event. At first I thought it was a good fence until I saw the agility displasei by the attendant. When I slw him go up I changed my mind. Herbert Jones, an attendant, raid that at 3.45, ho heard a whistle and a shout. He rushed to the pit but saw nothing but rings in the water. He vent to a farm and got a rope with which he dragged the pit. The bodies were recovered at, 4 20.

The Coroner: Has ITny-o-n-o- else tried to got Over the fence during the time you have been there ?—No. How long have you teen there ?—ix and a ball years. You know this patien: very well 7—Yes. Did he display any suicidal tendencies ?—No. The Coroner seTd it ars a sad thing that a patient should lose his life, but it was worse to think that a sane man should also lose his life in tryiug to rave him. He thought the jury would be satisfied that as far as the asylum authorities were concerned they thought it was a perfectly safe fence. They might take it that the committee would not allow the patients to walk the grounds if they knew there was any death trap. It would be by way of suggestion and not blame if the jury recommended that the fence should be recon- structed.

The jury deliberated in private and returned a verdict that Benyon committed snicide whilst of unsound mind, and that Thomas died in an heroic attempt to save the patient, such death being socidental. The Coroner : Do you make any suggestion about the fence?

The Foreman : We want to know if there were sufficient attendants for the number of patients exer- cising in the yard ? Mr Francomb said that one attendant to each 12 patients was considered a good working staff for out- side purposes. The jury recommended that the fence round the clay pit should be made absolutely unclimbable. The Coroner said he was informed that Dr Boyd, Dr Hopwood, and two clerks, named Mr F J Collins and Mr T W Uncles, ran to the pit when the accident occurred, and divesting themselves of some of their clothing made several attempts to bring the bcdies to the surface. The pit was twenty feet deep where the bodies wese found.

The jury warmly commended the courageous acts of the doctors and the clerks, Mr Francomb expressed appreciation on behalf of the committee of the bravery of Thomas, who clearly died in the performance of his duty. Sympathy was extended to the mother, who was the only relative of Ishomas, who was a zealous officer, extremely geni3l, and highly popular with everyone with whom he came into contact at the asylum. As far as monetary com- pensation could go they would not shrink from making good the loss, and in carrying out the jury's recom- mendation. They also appreciated the heroic attempts of the doctors and the clerks in their gallant attempts to save the lives of the unfortunate men. The jury's recommendation would have the best attention of the Committee.

--- On Monday evening the staff attended a memorial service in the Asylum Chapel. An address was given by the chaplain, the Rev S W Chadwick. Mr J W Taylor, presided at the organ and at the beginning of the service he played Chopin 's Funeral March and after the service the Dead March in "Saul."

DISGRACE TO THE NATION. PROF. MOORE AND PEOPLE'S TEEM

Professor Benjamin Moore, of the Liverpool tiniver- sity, lecturing at Wallasey on Monday said the condition of the people's teeth at the present day was a disgr .ce to the British nation, About 70 per cent, of the popu- lation had defective teeth, including a great number of their little girls, whose teeth, from the pure point of view of ornaments, ought to to kept good. From another standpoint there was nothing so important in the body for producing poisons than damaged end deca3log teeth. Those poisons were drawn into the system, and antemia was the result. It was to be hoped that the parents of the present generation would do more for posterity in that respect than had been done. Professor Moore expressed the opinion that tuberculosis killed about one person out of every six or seven, and it generally arose from bad housing or insanitary con- ditions. Here they stood as a nation face to face with that question which science knew perfectly well how to prevent if given a free band and sufficient money, Tet they did nothing.
