LYMM.

HOCKEY MATCH.—LYMM LADIES' V URMSTON LADIES (IST TEAM).—A good game was witnessed on Saturday on the ground of the former club, the result being a draw and the score 4-4. Ideal weather and excellent ground conditions, coupled with the thorough sporting spirit of the opposing teams, made up a most enjoyable hour's contest. Both sides sustained a hard tight and did not relax efforts throughout, though in strict justice one must say the Urmston ladies were occupied a good part of the time in the defence of their goals. On the Lymm ladies team special mention shoulo be made of Miss Guest (centre forward) for the verve which she puts into her attack and shots. Misses Dorothy Pearson and R Oddie did good work in running up to the wings. Miss Mabel Kirkpatrick acted with commendable judgment as captain.

CHERRY-LANE HA RVEST FESTIVAL.—The harvest thanksgiving services of the Independent Methodist Chapel, Cherry-lane, were held on Sunday and Monday last. Mr W Bellamy, of Leigh, was the preacher planned, but owing to illness his place had to be taken by Mr Briggs, also of Leigh, whose preaching delighted all despite the straight but unpleasant truths that he drove home. The interior of the chapel was decorated with flowers, fruit, vegetables, corn, and several loaves of bread, there being a profuse display of chrysanthe- mums and dahlias. The decorations were the work of Mesdames R Hewitt, W Mercer, %V Mort, T Readitt, Miss Mercer, Messrs R Hewitt, H Readitt, T Readitt, and Masters H Readitt and F Wright. On Monday evening Mr W Mort presided over a meeting at which the Rev R W B Whiteway gave a lecture on Garibaldi. After Mr Mort had thanked Mr. Whiteway for his lecture the andience adjourned to the schoolroom where the fruits, flowers vegetables &c. were sold by auction. The attendance at all the meetings was very satisfac- tory and the collections which were for the Trust Funds were also very good.

RECITAL AT THE WESLEYAN SCnOOL.—Mies Minnie M Mitchell, of Manchester held a recital in the Wesleyan Sunday School, on Wednesday night. There was a good attendance to hear her programme which consisted of thirteen miscellaneous items varying in authorship from Guy Boothby to Tennyson. Drama, pathos, meloay, and humour were all represented anti found efficient treatment at the hands of Miss Mitchell. The popular item appeared to be the humorous recital " Mrs Bateson's Tea Party " (E Thorneycroft Fowler), with the dramatic" Mad Marie "(II Childe-Pemberton) and the descriptive piece " Enoch on the Island ' (Tennyson) as close favourites. A musical monologue " Dickens," which was accompaned by Mrs Frank Brown, also received a good share of the applause. Owing to the pressure of time three items had to be withdrawn from the programme. The recital was con- cluded by singing" God save the King."

FESTIVAL AT TILE CIIERCII.—The Parish Church Harvest Festival Services were conducted on Sunday. Holy Communion was celebrated at 7 and 8 a.m. whilst Matins and Litany were beld later in the morning, the Rev E D Wray, Vicar of Timperley being the preacher In the afternoon the Sunday school children marched to the church where they were met by the choir who led them twice round the interior, the children deposit- ing their gifts of fruit and flowers at the font as they passed along. After taking their seats Captain Pickersgill, of the Church Army, conducted the fruit and flower service, giving the children an instructive, interesting, and topical address. In the evening the Rev and Hon C F Cross, vicar of Stretton, preached an excellent sermon to the crowded congregetion. The anthem with the bass solo taken by Mr Bowen, was confidently given under the direction of Mr William Cooke,F.R.C.O. The sacred edifice was chastely decorated with flowers, plants, corn and fruit, the altar,chancel, pulpit and font ornamentations being much admired, as was one of the central windows in the south wall. The work of embellishment was carried out by Mesdames Burrows, Callender, Farrar, Newmarch, Palmer, Watkin and Watson ; and the Misses Boumphrey, Callender, Duncan, Gregsoo,Hartley,Hill,Mouldsworth and Norris.

MR JAMES BERNARD AT TBE PRIMITIVE MF.TIIO- - CHURCII.—The members of the Primitive Methodist Church organised a recital for Wednesday night last. The schoolroom, which had been tastefully and comfortably adorned, was packed with an enthusiastic audience. The duties of the chairman were carried out by Mr G L Welford who, in a brief speech, stated his pleasure at being able to introduce Mr Bernard, the reciter, to a Lymm audience- Judging from the titles of the pieces on the programme, he thought they should have an enjoyable time. Mr Bernard gave excellent interpretations of " Stories, Scenes, and Sketches of Life and Character."—A man of rare intellect, whose prodigious memory combined with natural dramatic gifts, enables him to enthral his audience from the beginning to the end of the recital—Mr Bernard opened with the old German legend "The Pied Piper of Hamelin," and he invested the story with a spirit of realism that peopled the stage with actual living characters :—The pompous mayor ; the mystical piper; and rats, cats' everywhere. The applause was unstinted and thoroughly merited. The two sketches of Scotch Peasant Life which followed : "A Nippy Tongue " and " Drumsheugh's Love Story" both written by lan MacLaren, were in a totally different vein but were equally meritorious. Humour and pathos alternated in delightful contrast. The wielodic poem " The Bells " (Edgar Allen Poe) depicting Life's story of youth, love, sorrow and death, was given in a realistic manner. The study of old age, " Old Grime's Masterpiece " (G W Edwards), was a consummate exposition of simulated senility. The impersonations of the youthful student and the old artist were dramatically portrayed. The story of the old painter's ambition to I paint a picture of the Christ, and his search for a suit- able face which lasted until his last moments, when the veil is rent and he sees the face of the Saviour, carried the audience breathlessly along. Another good item was Mr Bernard's reminiscences of experiences that have occurred to him whilst reciting at various places. The concluding contribution was the character sketch " Minikin, Mr Lot and I " (from " Paul Kelvar," special permission to recite the piece having been given by the author, Mr Jerome IC Jerome), which evoked roars of laughter and necessitated the reap- pearance of Mr Bernard, who tendered his thanks in a characteristic manner. The recital was enriched by the singing of Mr A D Thomas, of Altrincham, whose solos and encores consisted of " The bells of gold," "An old garden," " Dear little jemmy face," and " You'd better ask me," which were well received. Mr Hill proved an efficient accompanist. The singing of the National Anthem brought the meeting to a close.

A TRAGIC OCCURRENCE.--It is with deep regret that we have to inform our readers of another sudden death in the L)mm district, the deceased being Mr George Arthur Holt, of Thelwall Brook. Mr Holt's residence, which is at the junction of the Warrington to Statham and Lymm roads, is very popular with cyclists, Mrs Holt's reputation of excellent catering being a well-established fact. Mr Holt was a signal- man on the local branch of the L and N-W Railway, being stationed at the time of his death at the Wilders- pool Crossing Box. On Saturday night last he was on duty and just as he was preparing to leave the house for NVarrington he heard a loud crash in the roadway. Hurrying out to see what was the matter he found at the bend of the road the Royal Mail van, which travels between Lyrnm and Warrington, turned over on its side. It appears that the driver of the van which was approaching the curve at a high speed, thought he heard a conveyance coming from the opposite direction and in order to avoid a collision be attempted to pull the horse up. The horse, however, swerved to one side with the result that the van toppled over. Fortunately the driver was unhurt and so when Mr Holt arrived they endeavoured to, and ultimately succeeded in rais- ing the van. As soon as the horse was liberated it ran away, the driver going in pursuit. Mr Holt stopped two cyclists who were passing and asked them to get the mail contractor to send out another horse, but after waiting some time and thinking that perhaps the cyclists would not deliver his message, he went to

Warrington himself. As he was approaching Om town Mr Holt felt a pain across his cheat but after visiting the contractor's stables went on to Wiluerspool crossing and took his turn in the box. Another conveyance was sent out and brought the mail bags to the G.P.O On returning home on Sunday morning be found the mail driver with a horse taking the empty van away, and laughed with him over the previous night's escapade. Mr Holt went to bed for the morning but got up for dinner. Whilst he was sitting in a chair in the kitchen he complained of a chest pain and called to his wife, but ere she reached him he fell back dead. Dr Thorpe was sent for but found that his services would be of no avail. Mr Holt, who was about forty years of age, bad hem under the doctor a little over twelve mouths on account of his heart, but bad not felt any ill effects due to it for the past year until Saturday night. It was thought that the strain of lifting the mail van was the cause of the failure. Two of Mr Holt's brothers, both railway men, have been the unfortunate victims of serious and in one , Case fatal accidents. One was killed by a train at Thelwall Station, whilst the other, who was acting as a guard on a train at Liverpool in the early part. of the year,tripped over some mail bags and had his arm taken off by the fast coach of his train. The funeral of the late Mr GA Holt took place on Thursday at Lymm Church. The Rev E P Wethered conducted the servioe. The chief mourners were :--"ihA widow, brothers and sisters, other relatives and a band of fellow railway , men from Warrington and district. There was a large I number of beautiful wreaths.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 12, 1912.

A FAMILIAR FIGURE LEAVING WARRINGTON.

FAREWELL TO MR. AND MRS. H. E. TONGE AND FAMILY.

On Tuesday evening a concert and social evening was held at the Independent Methodist Church, Grappen- hall to commemorate the departure of Mr Tonga and family who have left the district for Australia.Mr Tonge has acted as conductor of the Grappenhall Methodist Church Choir for the past four years During that time he has made many friends and his services were very highly appreciated by every member of the Church and congregation. Councillor It Henshall occupied the chair, and Mr It H Wood (Connexions! Evangelist) took part in the proceedings. The following artistes helped to make the meeting the huge success that it was t—Mr Hadkiss solo ; Master Harold Henshall, a recitation ; Miss C Shakeshaft, song ; Miss Lydia Foden, recitation ; Mrs Warburton, song ; Miss Lilian Tonge, recitation ; Mr Warburton, song ; Mr Thomas Lean (who created much merriment with his humorous readings) ; Mr and Mrs Warburton, duet ; and Miss Marion Lewis, solo Miss Shakeshaft acted as the accompanist.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION TO MR. TONGE.

_ During the interval Mr Tonge was the recipient of two handsome presentations. Mr U Leah (organist), in very suitable terms made a presentation to Mr Tonge on behalf of the choir, and said how very sorry they were to lose his services. He (Mr Tonge) had been of invalu- able assistance to them. This present took the form of a folding pocket baton, beautifully mounted and suit- ably inscribed. On behalf of the leaders and members of the church Mr Henshall presented Mr Tonga with an inlaid rosewood eight days' clock. This also bore a suitable inscription. Mr Henshall spoke with much feeling about Mr Tonge's services to their church. He had not only acted as their conductor, but bad been a very willing worker in raising funds towards their proposed new church, and on two special occasions he had raised over IN by his efforts. Mr Henshall said that it would be difficult to fill Mr Tonge's place. On behalf of the church he wished Mr and Mrs Tongo and family a safe voyage, , good health, and future prosperity. On Sunday last Mr. Tonge's three daughters received beautifully bound Bibles as a present from the teachers of the Church. Mr. Tonge' in very suitable terms, thanked all for the beautiful tokens he bad received, and said that he should always be reminded of their great kindness. He urged the choir to follow their good work, and in doing so they would help to make the work of the church a success in their midst. -

Mr. Tongs has also been the recipient of a presenta- tion from the Warrington Male Choral Union, of which he was a member. This took the form of a beautifully engraved solid silver cigarette case, bearing his initials. Mr. Tonge and family sail for Australia on October 17th.

MOORE.

A WELL-KEPT STATioN.—Mr. George Tanner, station- master at Moore, is to be congratulated upon winning the third prize offered by the London and North- Western Railway Company, in the station garden competition, 1912, for the large district, bounded on the north by Preston, on the south by Stafford, on the west by Liverpool, end on the east by Manchester. The prizes are given for the best kept railway banks, the best display of flowers and the general cleanliness of the waiting rooms and platforms.

HARVEST FESTIVA L.—The harvest thanksgiv'ng ser- vices were held in the Church of St. Margaret and All Hallows on Sunday. There was a gocd attendance at each service. A children's t.ervice was held in the afternoon, when Mr. Stanslield gave a very interesting address. He said that this was a good example for them ; they must make their lives both beautiful and useful. Ile also pointed out that when anyone was sending flowers or fruit, they always sent their best. This was another example. They must give of their best to God, best of their time and best of their lives. The church was crowded at the evening service, the preacher being the Rev. E W Clarke, ot Stockton Heath. He took for his text Amos viii, 1. Special music was sung by the choir. WESLEY GUILD.—The first devotional meeting of the winter session was held in the Church on Monday evening and took the form of a Communion service, a good attendance being present. A very suggestive address was given by the Bev E Morgan from the words "What mean ye by this service" ? Speaking of the attitude of the two great sections of the Christian Church—Protestant and Romanist—towards the question of the real presence of Christ in the Eucharist, he said the conception in each case was identical, except that Protestants denied that the presence is anything hut purely spiritual. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

CRIPPLE'S PASTIMES.

At Runcorn County Court, on Tuesday, his Honour Judge Stanger heard an application made by Joseph Astbury. farmer, Halton, Runcorn, for tbe reduction of compensation payable to Samuel Rawlinson, a young farm labourer. _ _ _ . . . .

Rawlinson was injured rather badly by being crushed between a gate and a runaway horse in June, 1910, and he had since been paid compensation at the rate of lOs a week. A reduction was asked for on the ground that the damaged hip from which he had suffered had so far recovered that he was able to do light work. The respondent said that when be attempted the work given him by Mr Astbury he suffered great pain, and on the first day it took him an hour and a quarter to walk home, a distance of a mile. He denied having taken part In a set of lancers at a local dance a short time ago. He only played cards there. He admitted doing a bit of rabbit shooting, and that he had been out with the " guns" shooting on the Norton Estate. His Honour said he believed the youth to be still suffering from his injury but capable of doing some light work. The award must be reduced to 6s. weekly,

THE THREE R'S ENOUGH. COLONEL HALL WALKER ON EDUCATION.

Colonel W. Hall Walker, M.P., presiding on Monday evening at the yearly prize distribution in connection with the Little Wootton and Much Woolton Higher Education Classes, held at the Gateacre Institute, when Mr. William Hewitt (Director of Technical Education in Liverpool) gave an address. The Chairman spoke of the value of technical educa- tion, and said it was a valuable method of imparting knowledge to young people. It was far better that such knowledge should be thoroughly imparted to the young by their parents, as in olden times ; but there was a great tendency nowadays to take little interest in the actual trade which a man followed, for the reason that in the factories and work- shops the individual was not actually responsible for the completion of the article, and he only had a know- ledge of making a certain part of it. He was a Conser- vative and an old Tory, but he still thought that the learning of the three R's was education enough for those in particular spheres of lifeA Present day education was tending to equip them as clerks or schoolmasters. Mr W Hewitt deplored the lack of a spirit of self- denial and self-sacrifice in the pursuit of continued education such as was shown some years ago. There were students who understood the value of determined effort, but be did not know what they were going to do with the " tired " people who could not work. He bad read of an American experiment of electrifying a class, and, according to the report, the results were encourag- ing, but he would feel inclined to wait a little longer before recommending it to his committee in Liverpool. They had heard American stories before. Colonel Walker then distributed the prizes to the successful students at the last session. WIDNES TOWN COUNCIL. INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT.

At the Widnes Town Council on Tuesday, the Mayor (Alderman F Neil) presiding. Alderman Smith reviewed the minutes of the Gas and Water Committee. He stated that the consumption of these commodities was going up by leaps and bounds owing to the prosperity of the borough, and they would have to keep a very strict outlook upon the various properties if the Increase was maintained. A large works from the Midlands was about to set up in the tom), and they would be large consumers of water. In answer to Councillor Lewis as to the water capacity, the Chairman said so far as the prevalence of water was concerned they had an unlimited supply. There was enough water for a town three times the size.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DITTON DISASTER.

Alderman Smith said that the work of the local fire brigade bad been overlooked at the Ditton railway disaster, but be desired to give them every credit for their efforts to alleviate human suffering. What would have happened if the whole of the carriages had fired Was dreadful to contemplate. The brigade did a noble duty.

===== 0002601_19121012_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
HOSPITAL SUPERVISION.

Councillor Miller, during the progress of the Health Committee's minutes, mentioned a complaint be had received, and asked if a fully-qualified assistant was left in charge at the Accident Hospital during the absence of the matron. On a recent date, he continued, a man went there suffering from an accident, and there was no one in charge to render first aid. Consequently the men's lite was endangered through loss of blood. Councillor G Davies said he knew nothing about the occurrence, but he would make inquiries.

AS CRITICS. Councillor Garghan dealing with the gas minutes, said Widnes boasted Of its cheap gas. He did not think it was cheap, and he drew the committee's attention to the dim lighting of the streets in West Bank and Waterloo wards. It was all right to have nice fittings, butt were they getting the 1798 candle power? A farthing candle would give more light. Owing to bad light a chapel at the river end of the town was putting in electric light. Councillor Miller asked for slot-meters to be placed under the supervision of a weights and measures inspector. The committee were the vendors and testers of the machines, and he did not think it was right. He also complained about a letter having been sent by the gas engineer dealing with a matter that was fully discussed at the last meeting, and he protested against the infringement of what he described as the liberty of an official entering into a discussion by corres- pondence. Alderman Smith replied that when the quality was given as 17.03 it was so, and they could talk from then until the end of the world, and then not take the quality down. When members could come prepared with some scientific knowledge of gas the committee would listen to them. Complaints to the proper quarter would be dealt with, but he deprecated the use of the Council chamber. NEXT YEAR'S MAYOR.

At the conclusion of the business. the Council asked Alderman F. Neil to continue in office as Mayor, and he agreed to take over the duties for a third year.

ST. HELENS.

DIGNITY OF LABOUR. LORD JUSTICE KENNEDY AT ST. HELENS.

Lord Justice Kennedy on Monday night distributed the prizes to the students of the St. Helens Technical School, and Evening Continuation Schools, at the Town Hall. Colonel W. W. Pilkington, chairman of the Education Committee' presided over a large gathering. Alderman Martin, chairman of the Higher Education Committee, in his yearly statement, said there were 934 individual students in the Technical School, 95 in the School of Art, and 861 in the Evening Continuation Schools, which showed an increase on the previous year. About 2,000 of their young people were availing themselves of the opportunities presented 1.4 them in the Gamble Institute and in the Evening Continuation Schools. In the Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes examinations St Helens carried off most of the prizes. One feature of the year had been the special classes for miners, which during the summer were attended by 350 miners. Altogether 349 miners had been examined, of whom all passed save one. Lord Justice Kennedy remarked that' it was twenty years since he first addressed a large meeting in St. Helens. St. Helens might say that, whatever elre might have been the chances of life or of business, it had 'since 1896 made progress in the best of all directions founding, administering, and appreciating the greatest gift that could be given to the young, a sound and useful education. Inclastrial work required as much mental accuracy and intelligent thought as most kinds of clerical or literary avocations ; but the great thing to him was that the education given in their technical school was so wide. By having industrial training and pure mental training side by side in the same institution it tended to get rid of the thing which they ought to get rid of—namely, the false idea that there was a superior rank in the

non-manual industries or occupations. It led to a just recognition of the dignity of skilled labour, and the perception that the work of a skilled artisan was as worthy as that of a clerk, and certainly, to his mind, not less stimulating to the intellect. In his judgment, one of the greatest dangers in the development of education nowadays was the excessive early specialisation. The studies of the young ought to be as broad as they could, so that they might have their minds trained not only in accuracy and precision, but also in imagination. Imagination ought to be cultivated far more than it sas. It was the want of imagination that made men and women unsympathetic with others, because they did not put themselves in their places. It made one nation for want of imagina- tion unsympathetic towards other nations. In regard to the joys of life, an unimaginative person, while perfectly accurate and correct in judgment, remained cold to the things of beauty and of art, and life for them lost one half, not only of its pleasures, but of its reality. (Applause.) MR. RIGBY SWIFT AND ULSTER.

Mr. Rigby Swift, M.P. for St. Helens, speaking at Sutton on Saturday night, said he had never received a more emphatic expression of opinion than he had at the meetings at which he had discussed the Home Rule question during the week. The people echoed the sentiments he expressed and approval for Ulster in their determination to have Home Rule at no price and under no circumstances. (Applause.) He asked if there had been any weakening on the part of the people in hesitat- ing at the separation of Ireland from Great Britain. (No.) Did they (Ind any relaxation of the people's determination to be no party to the splitting up of the Empire ? He was satisfied that never in the whole course of the history of the Unionist party had it been more solid than it was at present. (Applause). He found upon every hand that St Helens would be no party to the policy of Home Rule which had been introduced by the Government at the dictation of Mr John Redmond,and he found every- where a sympathy with the demand of the men of the north of Ireland that this thing should not be forced upon them, a sympathy with their request that they should be allowed to remain in the Union, under the protection of the British Constitution, a sympathy which gave him authority to say that to whatever extremity these men might be driven in their endeavour to remain under the British flag they would receive the support and the assistance of the Conservative and Unionist party in St Helens. (Applause.)

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Monday, there being present : Messrs W Thompson (chairman), W Latham, W Goslin, E L Bent, CB F Borron, W Davies, T Ritchie, W Coiling- wood, R T Evans, W Roughley. J Sankey, C Cole (clerk), A Bowes (surveyor), it T Surtees (engineer).— The Gas Committee met prior to the Council and con- sidered the question of the vacancy in the staff at the gas works. It was decided to appoint Mr Surtees gas engineer and manager ; Mr T Swift assistant gas manager and also stores clerk, Mr Swift being required to occupy the house and to have house, coal and gas free in addition to his present salary.— The Surveyor submitted to the committee the detailed plans and estimates for a oomprehensh e scheme of sewage treatment to satisfy the requirements of the Local Government Board, in accordance with the general outline already approved. He estimated the total cost, exclusive of land and wayleaves, to be .£9,310, made up as follows :—Vitriol Square Works, £l4O ; Central Works—additions, £4,240 ; Vulcan Works, £2,660 ; West-end Works—additions, £2,270 ;or a total of £9,310. It was resolved that the plans and estimates be approved, and that application be at once made to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of a sum of a £lO,OOO, to cover the estimated cost and the purchase of land, &c.

DOMESTIC SERVANTS AND THE

INSURANCE ACT.

ro TIIE EDITOR OF THE" EXAMINER."

Dear Sir,—May I venture to point out through the medium of your valuable paper that there are still many thousands of domestic servants who come within the provisions of the National Health Insurance Act, and who have yet failed to enrol themselves as members of an Approved Society. It is in the highest degree desirable that these should be advised as to their position, and made to realise the unfortunate condition in which they will be placed as Post Office contributors in the event of prolonged illness or permanent disablement. A Post Office Contributor who suffers a break down in health will soon exhaust the deposit credited to her account in the Post Office and be left without any further provision, whereas a member of an Approved Society in such circumstances will be able to demand Sickness Benefits for twenty-six weeks, and Disablement Benefit so long as the illness continues, the benefits in either case being, of course, subject to the waiting periods under the Act. All those who have at heart the interests of this important class of wage earners, would do well to advise them not to neglect the valuable opportunities which the Act has provided for them, but which can only be secured by becoming members of an Approved Society. It is recognised that owing to the conditions under which the majority of Domestic Servants are employed, the ordinary benefits provided by the Act are not the best that could have been designed, but suitable alter- „alive benefits are being brought forward. The scheme which appears most likely to benefit Domestic Servants is that suggested by the Prudential Approved Society for Domestic Servants whereby in exchange for a slightly reduced rate of benefit during the first six weeks of illness the benefit is increased for the following 20 weeks from 7s lid to 13.. This scheme though Dot yet sanctioned by the Insurance Com- missioners, is still before them and if approved will be available as an alternative to the ordinary benefits at the option of the member. Domestic servants are generally regarded as having a very favourable health experience and they should therefore join a society which is restricted to members of their own calling, as by this means their lighter sickness rate will result in increased benefits. The Prudential • Approved Society for Domestic Servants is by far the largest society, the membership' of which is absolutely restricted to female domestic servants, and whatever scheme of alternative benefits is I found to be most suitable will be put forward at the earliest possible date. As regards the organisation available for doing the necessary work, it is sufficient to say that there are over 20,000 representatives already calling at practically every house in the kingdom to attend to the 20,000,000 life assurance policies in force and this organisation will afford the members the greatest possible convenience. While so far from spending annually the 3s Sd per head permitted they will undertake the work for 2s 6d, thus guaranteeing in advance an important economy for the benefit of the members. It only remains to add that the Prudential Approved Societies are conducted entirely in the interests of the members, to whom all profits belong.— Yours faithfully, A. C. THO'MPSON, Chairman of Committees, Prudential Approved Societies.

A BURGLAR'S MESSAGE. Thomas Smith (alias " Ginger " Smith) (42), labourer, at the Cheshire Quarter Sessions, was found guilty of receiving a silver match-box, silver brooch, Sr.c., the proceeds of a burglary at Warrington Golf Club, between July 26 and 27. Inspector Breeze said when he examined the premises after the burglary he found the following message left in the smoking-room: "God helps those who help themselves !" The prisoner who had previous convictions for housebreaking and shop- breaking, was sentenced to nine month's imprisonment.

le Single Tax."

Asquith speaking at Ladybank on Saturday last, said in reference to the land policy of thm Government, "Whatever the proposals of ,the Government may be, they will not embrace what is called the policy of • the Single Tax ' ; which, to my knowledge, has not a single supporter in the present Cabinet, and which, in my opinion and I believe in the unanimous opinion of my colleagues also, by singling out, for the purpose of bearing the whole burden of taxation, of tne land—to the exclusion and relief of all other forms of property—is consistent neither with justice nor with expediency." It is a pity the term " ingle Tax" was ever intro- duced into this country. It is all very well in a land where the national revenue is derived from customs' duties on almost every article entering the country,for the advocates of the taxation of land values to propose to sweep away all the articles on the tariff and sub- stitute for all these One Tax on land values. It is very different in Britain where there are many carefully thought-out direct taxes, everyone of which is a good method of raising revenue. It is far better to keep to the policy of taxing land values, and, with the proceeds of that tax, we can abolish some of the others • beginn- ing with those— such as the Sugar Tax—which are taxes on the food of the people. This policy is and has been for many years a part of the Liberal Programme. The tax must be extended to agricultural land and also used as a source of local revenue. I would remind your readers tnat when in 1806 the Conservative Government passed the Agricul- tarsi Rates Act to give relief in rates on agricultural land, apart from buildings, Sc. a few weeks was all that was deemed necessary to find the value of the agricultural land ; when the proposal is to impose a rate upon this value, we are told it is impossible to find the value. _ . .

The advocates of the taxation of land values insist that by their scheme the opportunity for making huge fortunes will pass away, since these fortunes are only obtained because labour is deprived of its proper reward, by the land monopoly. The Housing Ques- tion, to which Mr Asquith referred especially, is a land question and the taxation of land values will go far to solve it. The tax imposed in Mr Lloyd George Budget has already had the effect of bringing into use many spaces that were being held up for a rise in value, and the building trades have been greatly benefited thereby. The whole question is dealt with in the Financial Reform Almanack for 1912-13," which will be published in a iew days.—Yours faithfully, EDMUND K. MUSPRATT, Financial Reform Association. President.

Paltry Progress. Sir,—Although I admit the difficulty of seeing things from other people's point of view I am afraid that our little discussion about strikes is making very little headway. I may be wrong but I think the fault is not on my side. For instance I write a letter criticiz- ng our general outlook, including strikes. Mr Dukes replies but does exactly the same that he has done in his last letter, that is, makes a big cry about unity, solidarity Sic. Without analysing accurately the position of the worker and his relation to strikes, I try tc take a large view and discuss strikes from the position of when the workers are nationally organised, and argue that even then the workers cannot obtain economic freedom. Mr. Dukes says strikes are necessary to maintain a bare subsistence and if the workers were only properly organised they could by means of a strike obtain economic freedom. Personally, I say strikes are not necessary to maintain a bare existence or by the power of a national strike win for the workers economic freedom. In the first place I believe the present state of society, that is the ownership of the means of production for the benefit of a few, decides the material status of the workers', that is, the total wealth production of the community goes to those , who own the means of production, minus just sufficient to keep those who do riot own any means of wealth production. But, says Mr Dukes, if we organise our labour power and refuse to produce, the worker can obtain economic freedom. I contend that money being the source of exchange, the capitalist having more money than the worker therefore can stand a strike longer than the worker. Strikes to me are the explosion of the accumulated moral forces known as discontent, ivh;ch sometimes succeed in regulating some evils. But because of this we must not mistake the strike as an economic power. It has none. The French revolution comes nearest to beingea national strike that there ever has been, but the workers in France are still enslaved by a dominant class. "•

It may be unfortunate but I do not believe that the whole of the people will develop to a certain stage of economic intelligence and then one bright morning— hey presto —change the whole course of business. I hope Mr. Dukes will not misjudge me. Says Stephen Blackpool to Mr. Bounderby "The strong hand will never do't. Agreeing fur to mak one side unnat'rallly swine and for ever right, and tother side unnat'rally swine and for ever wrong, will never, never do'ty. Most o' RNA, ratin"em as so much Power, and regulatin 'em as if they was figures in a room, or machines, wi'out loves and likeins, wi'out memories and inclinations, wi'out souls to weary and souls to hope—when aw goes quiet, draggin on wi'em as if they'd nowt o'th ...kind, an when aw goes onquiet, reproachin"em for their want of each humanly feeling."

I appreciate the indignity of the workers' situation. I have suffered it all from destitution onwards. I also hope I appreciate the value of the various methcds necessary for the workers' emancipation. At present I do not believe that wages raising is one of them. The workers must take steps to get control directly of the necessities of life. Wages are not a necessity. Good clothing and shelter are.—Yours • truly, LABOR OMNI& VINCIT. [Correspondence on this subject must now cease,—En 'Ex."]

LOCAL WEDDING.

Dawson—lnce.

A pretty wedding took at St Peter's Church on Monday when Mr James Dawson, Bridgeo f Allan, son of Mr and. Mrs Robert Dawson, was married to Miss Jennie Ince, daughter of Mr and Mrs Thomas Ice, 81, Lythgoes-lane. The bride, who was given away by her father, was beautifully attired in a dress of ivory satin tnouseline, trimmed with chiffon and pearls. She wore a veil over a spray of orange blossom and white heather, and carried a bouquet of roses, lilies of the valley and white heather. She was attended by her sister, Miss Elsie Ince,who was prettily gowned in pale pink duchesse mouseline. Her black beaver hat was trimmed with a pink wing and she carried a bouquet of bronze chrysanthemums. She also wore a gold signet ring, the gift of the bridegroom. The Rev B Percival, vicar of St Peter's, officiated, assisted by the Rev James Collins, vicar of St Thomas's, Stockton Heath. Dr T Brownlie McKendrick acted as beet man. Suitable music was played by the organist, Mr Padmore. A reception was afterwards held at the Winmarleigh Cafe. Later in the day the newly married couple left for Keswick, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride travelled in grey whipcord costume, with a heliotrope velour hat and furs, the gift of the bridegroom.

" SHERWOOD."-MR. J. B. POOLE.

-MISS NELLIE MULLINS.

The annual amateur operatic enterprise of the War- rington Musical Society has hecme such a well- established and highly acceptable feature of our local musical life that we are apt to take it for granted. It is indeed difficult to realise that there ever was a time when lovers of this hi d of entertainment in Warring- ton had no opportunity of seeing the most delightful works of the light opera type presented by their own townspeople. From the very first year when some of the bolder spirits of the Musical Society carried the new project to success, one triumph has succeeded another until it has now become an agreeable annual task to congratulate the operatic section upon the enrichment of their fine record. And so it is this year. While we would not go so far as to describe the production of "Dorothy," which has attracted exceptionally large and appre- ciative audiences to the Royal Court Theatre this week, as the beat in the company's history—some of the earlier achievements undoubtedly excelled it in certain respects—all concerned may be justifiably proud of this latest undertaking. " Dorothy " has features which bespeak favourable reception sparkling music, picturesque action, a well-connected, fairly credible story and an abundance of gay, rollick- ing humour. It takes us back to that fascinating " old English " period of the mid-eighteenth century and, although perhaps casting an undue glamour over the rural life of the time, presents a picture with a much greater semblance of reality than those romantic happenings in an imaginary, topsy-turvey kingdom, so dear to the hearts of constructors of light opera and musical comedy. By transporting us to the Georgian era the choice of " Dorothy " provokes, to some extent, comparison with " Tom Jones," brilliantly staged by the society two years ago. Both operas treat a promising theme artistically, but the palm must be awarded to "Tom Jones." While Celliees work was the pioneer of its kiod and charms the ear by the graceful a hythm and airy tunefulness of its music, German has realized the old English atmosphere even more successfully and his composition has a depth and an emotional quality —especially in the tenser passages—which cause many melodies to linger in the memory. The score of "Dorothy " is, for the most part, light, almost to evanescence, but on the other hand the opera boasts one of the most popular of modern love songs in that happy after-thought "Queen of my heart." "Dorothy" is some- what disappointing in its opportunities for individual distinction. Practically the whole of the action revolves round five personages, and the other char- acters have comparatively meagre parts allotted to them. But against these disadvantages must be set the fact that the concerted music of" Dorothy" is not only considerable in quantity but it is excellent in quality and of exceptional interest to the listener. The opera is therefore as a whole uniformly pleasing if not exactly captivating and from this point of view it may be regarded as thoroughly worthy of the infinitude of painstaking work expended upon it by the society, and a decided improvement upon" San Toy," the signal success of last year. From the rise of the curtain on the first act we are made to feel that it is pleasant to be living in the "good old days." The jollity of the country-folk assembled for the hop-pickers' festival in the gardens of the Kentish hostelry has nothing artificial or forced about it. The whole of the company seem to abandon themselves to the spirit of the occasion in whole-hearted fashion. What a transformation to the magnificent ball room scene in Chanticleer Hall ! Here we have a veritable feast of colour, dazzling yet harmonious. The spacious hall, with its handsome oak staircases, makes an ideal, old- fashioned setting and as the guests, resplendent in rich satins and brocades, dance the stately gavotte, the

effect is exquisitely beautiful. Again a change comes over the scene, for the excitement of a mock burglary has barely died down, when into the old hall dash the red-coated huntsmen, who " Tally-ho" right lustily, while—to add the crowning tcuch of realism—a number of hounds, vigorously straining at the leash as if their quarry sought refuge beyond the f fotlighta,are brought forward, amid general enthusiasm. Mr Walford Lomax, the coach and stage manager, is a past master of the art of manipulating stage " crowds," and the result is seen in perfect grouping, natural animation —in short, in all that the word "go " implies. The dancing is a charming feature in itself and the per- formers have shown surprising aptitude in accustom- ing themselves to the unfamiliar measures of the old days. The cast includes the names of several favourites who have distinguished themselves in previous enter- prises and their re-appearance is cordially welcomed Mrs. C. Garton Stn e figures in the title part and she acts with such delightful freedom and naivete that one can only regret that her vocal powers are not commensurate with her undoubted histrionic ' gifts. Mrs. Stone is thoroughly at home on the stage ; there is nothing stilted or exaggerated in her actions, and she is the &Lib° liment of vivaciousness and tantal:zi,g coynEei. Her equipment as an !Tem principal does, however, fall short of the ideal in the respect above-mentioned for, although her vocalisation is sweet and refined,it is mucti too weak to be adequately effective. The same comments apply to a great extent to Miss Nellie Afullitm who, as Lydia, seems to revel in the artifices of the fair charmer intent upon testing the devotion of ardent lovers. She is a saucy yet winsome companion of D ,rothy, but like Mrs. Stone, she is hardly equal to the vocal demands of the part. The two ladies areseen at their best in the last act when, having challenged their lovers to a duel, their valour rapidly evaporates at the prospect of a real and possibly mortal combat. The struggle between pride and fear gives rise to some delightful by-play which is keenly relished by the audience. Mr. J. Babbitt adds to his laurels by a flue presentment of Geoffrey Wilder, the hero of the piece. What the opera company owes to Mr. Babbitt's artistic abilities it would be difficult to over-estimate and we are according him the highest praise when we say that his Wilder is worthy to rank with his most conspicuous triumphs of the past. He is the ideal wooer, manly, dashing and _impulsive, and he sings that appealing ballad " With such a dainty maid" with all his wonted tenderness and fervour. Mr. J B. Poole makes his first appear- ance in a leading r6le as Harry Sherwood, Wilder's bosom friend, and right well does he acquit himself. What little stiffnes was observable in the early stages of the opening performance was soon overcome and Mr. Poole infused the requisite animation and enthu- siasm into his acting. A"Lcally, his great opportunity comes in that favourite song " Queen of my heart' and in this he rises to the occasion, his rendering being most sympathetic and expressive, and gaining a nightly encore. In the concerted music in which the four lovers are associated, the ladies' voices are considerably over- weighted uy the gentlemen's, and accordingly the balance of the parts is often far from being all that could be desired. The life and soul of the opera in the sphere of merry-making, is Mr G H Smith as Lurcher, the highly original Sheriff's officer. Any part with humorous possibilities is safe in Mr Smith's hands and be succeeds in making Lurcher an irresistibly amusing personage. His self-possession is something to marvel at, and he keeps the audience in a roar by the facility with which he reels off jests and witticisms so up-to-date that even the insurance stamp provides one shaft from his armoury. Lurcher's ludicrous ttorhols in the ballroom, when more or less dexterously negotiating the train of his

LLIS."—MRS. E. A. SANDERS. Photos by J. Parkinson.

lady partner, are in striking contrast to the dignified demeanour of all around him, while his love-making t the widow would move the most stolid to mirth. Lumber is certainly one of Mr. it mith's greatest hits in operatic comedy parts. Madame Lily Fairhurst is an attractive Phyllis Tuppitt, the village maid, and deserves special commendation for an artistic inter- pretation of the ballad "The time has come," her enunciation being particularly g od. Every word of the song is declaimed with the necessary expression and can be distinctly heard by all, the number calling forth hearty appreciation -each evening. Mr. J. E. Hannan makes the most of the part of Tom Strutt, the yokel who marries Phyllis, —in fact he is, for the time being, a model of bucolic simplicity. Mr. Fred Fieldhouse is well placed as Squire Bantam, the hale and hearty host, his con- ception of the character being the very embodiment of joviality and good cheer. " With a welcome to all " is a song after his own heart and he gives it with rare gusto. Mrs E W Clarke, as Mrs Privett, contributes largely to the humorous element in her breezy passages with Lurcher ; Mr Wallace Ray is a typical country inn keeper—bluff, outspoken and ' hospitable—Miss Lena Duckworth comports herself with befitting stateliness as L tdy Betty, while Mr A H Davis, as the parson, does what little is required of him quite satisfactorily. The chorus work is not a very strong feature of , " Dorothy" but the finale of Act II provides an oppor- tunity to realize an effective climax and here the full resources of the opera company are impressively brought into play. The voices are well-balanced but it cannot be said that the singing, as a whole, is characterised by the necessary crioness, precision and attention to detail. The orchestra under Mr. R. H. Pearson's leadership is occasionally wanting in perfect agreement between its various sections Lnd, consequently, the tone is not unimpeachable, but there is no modete obtrusion of the instrumental parts at the expense oi the vocalists. Mr. Frank H. Crossley, the society's able conductor, directs the whole of the forces engaged in the enterprise with customary, tact anu judgment. Of the labours of Mr R Walford Lomax" behind the scenes" it would be impossible to speak too highly. The completeness of the opera from the dramatic and spectacular standpoints is a fine testimony to the thoroughness and patience with which he has discharged his peculiarly onerous duties. In this connection a special meed of praise must be given to Mr. T. Robinson, of Liverpool, who is respon- sible for the "Dorothy " scenery, Probably on no Occasion in the history of local amateur opera have more beautiful and artistic stage surroundings contributed to a success than has the equipment in this instance. The beautiful costumes, which have been greatly admired by all who have witnessed the production, are by Messrs B J Simmons and Co., London, and the wigs and make-up by Messrs J H Fyans and Co., Liver- pool, and Messrs Birch Browne and Son, Warrington. Phe whole of the arrangements have been in the hands of a sub-committee of the Musical Society consisting of Messrs J W Bibby, F H Crossley, Fred Fieldhouse and G H Smith, with Mr Henry Bond as the enthusiastic and hard-working hon. secretary and treasurer. The hearty co-operation of the management of the Royal Court Theatre is, of course, indispensable in ensuring that everything shall run smoothly and it has, as on previous occasions, been readily given. Mr. A. M. Loader, the general manager, has keenly interested himself in the project arid invaluable help has also been forthcoming from Mr. H. L. Warburton the acting manager. This Saturday (afternoon) there will be a matinee and the closing performance will be given t

RELIEF AND RESCUE WORK.

INTERESTING MEETINGS AT BOLD-STREET CHURCH.

Many phases of the rescue, relief and preventive work done by the Manchester and Salford Mission were illus- trated at two public meetings held under the auspices of the Mission, in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday. In the afternoon Mrs Dodds presided over a large attendance. She was supported by the Revs S F Collier (President-Designate of Wesleyan Methodist Conference), H Williamson and W A Beckett, Gipsy Smith and Sisters Nellie and Edith.

The Rev S F COLLIER explained the purpose of the Mission, and said it was a strong arm of the great church of Methodism. . _ . . .

Mrs. DODDS spoke of the good work the Manchester Mission was doing, and of how much they owed to that institution. The work demanded the sympathy, help and prayers of every man and woman iu that church. (Hear, hear). Sts-rzn NELLIE gave an interesting address, in the course of which she said it was hard work to realise how some of our brothers and sisters were living. A look into the poorer areas of our large towns revealed the squalor and misery amongst which some of our fellow countrymen existed, bat still they found Christians there. We were living in an age when men and women were working more for Christ. They should stand aide by side to do all that lay in their power to protect the purity of their sisters and brothers. Some of the people with whom they came into contact revealed tales of sorrow and sin which only the Gospel could heal. (Applause). GIPSY SMITH who rendered the song "Hark, the Shepherd's voice I hear " very effectively, said they were inspired by the Gospel they preached to rescue and lift men and women from the lowest depths of misery, sorrow and vice, to decency and respectability. All the work for social perfection sprung out of the preaching of the Gospel to the great multitudes. An army equal to more than halt the population of Warrington came through the doors of the Central Hall, Manchester, in a year. The Man- chester Mission had a reputation all over the world. It was an inspiration to all the forward movements in the world, eneaged in that class of work. (Applause.) About £20,000 was spent annually and out of that Mr Colliers' own Church raised 10Id in the shilling. They wanted the people outside to contribute the other “d.There would be about 400 to 500 men and women under the wing of the Manchester Mission that night. They had a rescue home for men with 350 beds and there were 50 beds in the women's home.(Applause.) They had discovered that there were far more fallen men than women in the country. Their investigations revealed that for every one woman who went astray there were 50 men. Speaking of the good results of the Mission the speaker said that one man had gained his degree at the University. (Applause.) The churcliwas crowded in the evening when another interesting dieeting was held under the presidency of Mr 12 II Posnett, J.P. of Runcorn. He was supported by the Revs Collier, H Williamson, W A Beckett, Gipsy Smith, and Sisters of the People. The Chairman said they were proud of the Manchester Missions' achieve- ments and proud of the head of that most useful organisation. (Hear, hear.) Sister EDITH gave an inspiring address and spoke of the utility of the womeu's refuge and the good work it did.

The Rev S F COLLIER, who had a hearty reception said one of the first questions they were confronted with was : Is the mission necessary to-day ? They did not claim that all the work done should be mission work ; they felt that it was necessary to have churches and chapels well organised in order that they could , help those who had been trained in a religious

atmosphere to worship. At the same time he thought they would agree with him when he said that however vigorous the organised churches might le, there was a great deal of work to be done which they could not no. Tt,ere was a certain class of people which the churches could not touch and therefore it was absolutely necessary and essential that there should be an organisation like the Manchester Mission. (Hear, hear.) The Central Hall was formed in 1886 and there were now 41 Central Missious. (Applause.) Speaking of the late hows kept by chil- dren the speaker saici that they bad investigated in six thoroughraree in Manchester and found that on one night there were 1,200 girls between the ages of 13 and 11 out after 10 o'cloce,am id all the giggling, horse- play and foul language that one could imagine. The invitation of the Mission was •• Whosoever will, may come," for they had nu restrictions,aa they were out for the restoration of all, to fetter end purer lives. (Hear, hear.) Alter dealtng with their pr...c.-dure in regard to the saving of the won.en, the speaker said there were plenty of men out of work and he thanked God for ail the State wai trying to do. Social reformers were, however, fearfully wanting ic patience and were in a fearful hurry. The mission was going on steadily in the way of perfecting the condi- tions of mankind. It was only during the last few years that the nation and the churches bad wakened up at all to study the (preations affecting social reform. It was the dirty of every Christian to read and study the questions which were troubling the nation. They were learning more each year, and the country was growing better but there was more work to be done. They should try and make it possible for every man and woman to take the fir.3t step on the ladder which led to Heaven. (Applause.)

St Paul's Church.

The harvest festival services in connection with St. Paul's Church were commenced on Thursday evening when there was a large congregation. The church had been tastefully decorated with the usual harvest products. The preacher was the Rev. F. H. Gillingham, M.A., Vicar of Bordesley, Birmingham, the well-known Essex County cricketer, who based his remarks on Psalm xiv., v 5, "Happy is he that bath the God of Jacob for his helper and whose hope is in the Lord his God," and his discourse was attentively listened to. Referring to cricket and football the rev. gentleman said that there was a danger of over- estimating sport, especially from a spectator's point of view. Young men should be encouraged to play games rather than watch others doing so. In addition to the usual harvest hymns the anthem " 0 Give Thanks " was rendered in a creditable manner by the choir. The Magniticat was sung to a special setting by Eaton Faning. Mr. C. Wilkes presided at the organ.

THE MAYOR AND "THE TRADE." WELCOME TO LICENSED VICTUALLERS' CONFERENCE.

Nearly 100 delegates were present at the half-yearly meeting of the Lancashire and Cheshire District of the Licensed Victuallers' Defence League of England end , Wales, v.hicla was held at the Lion Hotel, Warriagton, on Thursday afternoon. The Mayon (Conucillor Dr. Joseph), attended to welcome the visitors. He thought that as chief citizen, he had a right to do so and he felt sure that those whom he stood for would feel pleased that he had taken that course. He bad in his time had a great deal to do with members of their League in that town and elsewhere and he must say be had formed a high opinion of their character, both as regarded their honour and their mode of life—in a great measure, almost generally, their temperate mode of life, which was a very important thing in their old and honourable calling. ills Worship proceeded to pay a high tribute to the municipal work-of Alderman John Evans whose knowledga of engineering made him such a valuable chairman of the Water and Gas Committees. In his (the speaker's) capacity of Mayor and chief magistrate, he had to attend many meetings and express opinions upon diverse and differing interests, He did not want to run with the hare and hunt with the hounds and it was right that he should say one word definitely as to his opinion with regard to his being present that day. Some who were against drink altogether might think that he ought not to be there, but he was only acting up to his principles when he said that he was pethctly right in being there and in expressing the opinion that the members of the association and of their calling were worthy and temperate men—often men who conducted their business, which was a per- fectly legitimate one, in a thoroughly conscientious manner, and that in itself was the hest guarantee in aiding the cause of temperance. (Hear, hear.) He thought they very often aided that cause a great deal more by conducting their business in a proper, legitik mate manner, than those who might not always temper- ately talk about it in the other direction. (Applause.) Moderation on both sides would, in his opinion, lead more to true temperance than contentious doctrines bandied about between one side and the other, (Applause.) Air ISAAC TURNER, of Liverpool, in the course of his presidential address, said there was some dissatisfaction in the minds of the ardent politicia workers in the ranks of their trade. There was no doubt about the necessity of their striving to upset the party that had oppressed them,but it was felt that there shouldbe some distinct hope of reward from the Opposition side. Their Secretary and Parliamentary agent had therefore been instructed to make inquiries as to wnether there was a probability of some decided opinions being expressed by the leaders of the Conservative party. We have been the hewers of wood and carriers of water to the Conservative party, continued Mr Turner, for some considerable time, and it is quite time that 'they declared what they intend to do for us and how far their sympathy is likely to go in case of their accession to power in the early future. It is the inten- tion of the leaders of the trade to try and extract from the leaders of the Conservative party some information NS to their intentions."

Engineers' Picnic. The Whitecross Wire Side engineers. held their annual outing on Saturday. The party journeyed to Liverpool, where they visited several of .the places of interest, and afterwards crossed the river to New Brighton, where some time was spent in visiting the various places of amusement. The party reached Warrington about ten o'clock. The arrangements were made by Mr F Massey.

"LYDIA.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT., LIVELY PASSAGES IN KING'S SPEECH DEBATE. Reform of the House of Lords.

There were some lively and amusing passages in the adjourned debate on the King's 6peech at the meeting of the Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society held in Cairo-street Schools on Thursday evening. The attendance was again very satisfactory and the increased interest shown in the Society's work augurs well for a successful session.

Councillor J HA \vTitorts (L., Montrose), who re-opened the debate.contended that the Tory grievance with regard to the Parliament Act was illusory. All that the Liberal party asked was that when a Liberal Government was in power it should have the same chance of passing its laws as a Conservative Govern- ment had.(applause.) With regard to the Insurance Act, tne Liberals were willing to beat any discredit that I might be attached to it, but he booed the Conserva- tives would have the common honesty to give them the credit that would certainly follow when the benefits were in full operation. The Conservatives talked of old age pensions for twenty years, but never gave them. He supposed their idea of progress would be to talk of national insurance for twenty years, and leave it at that. (Hear, hear' and laughter.) The " fiscal reform " forecasted in the King's Speech was, of course, their old enemy Protection. It was one of the ironies of the political situation that the party whose leader gave free imports should be seeking to undo his work. The PRIME MINISTER (Mr E A White) alluded to Mr Winston Churchill's recent fore-shadowing of a number of provincial Parliaments. " Why " be asked " should not Warrington have its own Parliament? And if Warrington is to have its own Parliament, why should not this Parliament be that Parliament ? (Laughter and applause.) I would remind you that we should then be in a position to vote ourselves salaries at somebody else's expense, and I hope these little suggestions will tend to increase the popularity and membership of the society." (Laughter). The LEADER OF TILE OPPOSITION (Mr J Jarman) : We should never get you out of office then. The PRDIE MINISTER, continuing said the Leader of the Opposition seemed rather envious of the King's Speech and adopted the common practice, on his side of the House, of assuming with a considerable amount of arrogance if not impudence, that all measures of reform should come from the Liberal party. He said the Conservatives blessed Liberal ideas but did not approve of their methods. "We certainly do not approve of your methods " com- mented the Prime Minister. " I can only describe them as methods of madness. We should never dream of attempting to pass an Insurance Act with the enormous complications of the present Act in the way that you did." As to fiscal reform, used in the right sense of the word, they did want Protection— protection against unfair competition. It was the fashion of the Liberals to talk of that principle purely from the point of view of the consumer. He granted that as a rule the Conservatives talked more from the standpoint of the producer, but he would like to remind them that the workingmen in this country were the greatest producers. He asked the Socialists whether, in the event of an industrial dispute, they would approve of the importation of cheap labeler frcm the Continent? (" No, no.") But that would be consistent with the principles of Free Trade. During the coal strike there was an outcry on the part of , labour that they would not have foreign coal imported. There again they departed from the principles of Free Trade. Was it not reasonable that some tax should be put on foreign goods to balance the charge imposed on ' the home manufacturer by the Insurance Act ? Mr P O'BRIEN (L., North St. Pancras), in the course of a breezy speech, said that when the Liberals were in power the House of Lords was there for the purpose of blocking or rejecting all legislation that came from the Liberal side. With regard to Home Rule, he recalled Mr Chamberlain's vain statement in 1885 : "We have buried Home Rule for ever, and we have given it a magnificent funeral." (Laughter.) The question had, however, been resuscitated at every election since, and they were told at the last appeal' to the country that every vote given for a Liberal was a vote for Home Rule. To-day the majority ol the Scottish members were in favour of Home Rule. All Wales was favourable to it, all the Labour members were pledged to it,and he had evea heard a Conserva- tive candidate declare that, if he were only sure that it would not affect the integrity of the Empire, it might be worth while to pass the Home Rule Bill so as to get rid of the subject altogether and have a square fight on tariff reform. (Laughter). Mr F Ism:I-Lwow) (C., Midlothian) said the Conserva- tives supported the principle of the Insurance Act, but they disagreed with the methods of giving the benefits. They said the schema ought not to be on a con•rioutory basis or, if it were, it should be on a better foundation than the present Act and its consideration ought not to be rushed.

Mr J DALBY (S' E. Bradford) said the workers at present had to toil to the last drop of their blood to fill the sumptuous tables for their rich brethren. The Socialists wanted to inspire them with a noble and manly desire to sit at the table themselves. Mr E ENGLAND (L., Warrington) : They will have indigestion. (Laughter). Mr DALBY, alluding to national defence, said the Socialists feared no invasion—not even a German one— because they saw across the North Sea a mighty host of people who were with them in heart and soul and who loathed the German military bureaucracy as much as they in England did. The ATTORNEY GENERAL (Mr W B Forshasv) painted a very gloomy picture of the present condition of England. Ile forces of Radicalism, he said, threatened to pull down to the ground this " grand and time. " Constitution of ours. Those forces, march- ing under the false banner of social reform, had terrorised the whole population of these islands with their so-called progressive legislation. (Opposition laughter., All classes of the community were sick and weary of the new tyranny of democratic despotism. The aristocracy and the middle classes were groaning under a burden of robbery—(Opposition laughter)—which masqueraded under the name of taxation. The working classes were sick to death of being promised bread and given a stone—(Socialist : " Hear, hears ")—of being bullied and policed in their private affairs. The greatest want of England to-day was not social reform, but social rest. The people wanted a political rest cure. (Laughter.) He would write against everyone of the reforms recommended in the King's Speech the word " moderation," as a warning that they must not endanger their political health by trying to out-do the Opp mition. They wanted less legislation and more government. (Laughter and a VOICE : " More police.") With the House of Lords in a mutilated condition in con- sevence of the passing of the Parliament Act, the centre of power in this great Empire lay in the House of Commons—(opposition applause)—and as the House of Commons was governed by the Cabinet, and the Cabinet was governed by Mr Lloyd George, and Mr. Lloyd George by John Redmond—(a voice : "And John Redmond by his wife" and much laughter)—they found that the supreme authority in the State lay in the hands of John Redmond (A voice: "King John "). It was a terrible thing—(A Nationalist : We are honoured, sir ")—that when the Radicals were in power Mr Redmond bad only to persist for the two years allowed by the Parliament Act to be able to inflict on the people of this:country any imprudent and revolutionary measure that he pleased (A voice : "D. n't forget that the people of Ireland are behind him—a big majority.") The Conservatives were determined that that should not happen in the future. They were going to repeal the Parliament Act which toadied to democratic despotism and substitute a Bill which would bring into existence a strong Second Chamber which would effectively check any further revolutionary legislation. (A Vows: : " Liberal legislation.") They would insist on the hereditary principle. The great- ness and power of this mighty Empire were embodied in a single person—(A VOICE : "John Redmond, you said ")—a person whose name was revered from Canada to Australia, and whose very existence in the Constitution depended on the hereditary principle. While they had breath in their bodies, they would defend the Monarchy against all attacks. (An OPPOSITION MEMBER SO Will We.") "If you are against the hereditary principle " retorted Mr Forshaw " You must be against the Monarchy." (Socialist applause). The Ovvernment were determined to defend private rights and not to perpetrate injustice in the course of their social reforms. Mr G TOULMIN (L.. Lincoln) said the member for Westhoughton (Mr G F Clarke) said, with regard to the Insurance Act, that he would put the whole of the nine- pence on the working man. He (the speaker) hoped he would tell his would-be constituents that, next time he had to appeal to them. A Warrington Tory who bed held offioe in the present Government had declared that the Insurance Act gave more benefits to the working classes than any other measure brought forward by any party. With regard to army reform there never was a greater scandal in the history of this country than the administration of the War Office by the last Tory Government. While professing friendship for the ' workers, the Tories ratified the Taff Vale judgment I which made trade unions practically useless and Sir ' Frederick Banbury had now brought forward a Bill to repeal the Trades Disputes Act. Mr E J RADFORD (C., Wirral) waxed indignant at a Socialist member's " hitter and coutemptuous " refer- ence to the British flag. He denied that the children in the schools were dragooned into saluting the Union Jack. (A SOCIALIST : "What about Empire Day "?) As a schoolmaster, he had never heard of a school where the spirit of militarism was inculcated. (A SOCIALIST : " History"). Mr J ELWELL (L., Wednesbury) spoke of his experiences in Protected countries. He found as bad slums in Germany as be had seen iu Liverpool and be did not think any of the Conservative members would like to live under the conditions existing there. Mr CHAMBERLAIN (S., Gorton) said the debate had afforded evidence of the" sham antagonism" between the two orthodox parties. The Socialists were there to be propagate revolution—too long had they tolerated the cry of reform. Mr C PLINSTON (C., Darlington) resented the Socialists' labelling of the well-to-do as "parasites "

USINESS HOURS 9 to 8 DAILY.

and said it was arrogant for them to assume that they were the only party who had the interests of the work- ing classes at heart. REFORM OF THE HOUSE OF LORDS. Next Thursday evening the Prime Minister (Mr E A White) will introduce a Bill to repeal the Parliament Act and reform the House of Lords. The Ennia provisions are : 1. The Parliament Act 1911 is repealed. 2. From the date of the passing of this Act the House of Lord 3 as a Legislative Chamber shall consist of Peers of Right, Appointed Peers, and Elected Peers. 3. The Peers of Right shall be the Princes of the Blood Royal. the Lords of Appeal, the Premiers of self" governing Colonies, and Imperial Representatives to b° selected in manner to be hereafter prescribed. 4. The Appointed Peers shall be 50 Peer + appointed under the King's sign manual at the commencement 01 every Parliament. 5. The Elected Peers shall be elected at the coot- mencement of every Parliament, 50 by the Peers. 50 139 the House of Commons, and one by each County Council In the United Kingdom. 6. Peers not entitled to sit in the House of Lords shall be qualified for election to the House of Commons 7. All matters on which the two Houses of , Perlis" ment shall be unable to agree, shall be referred to the people by way of referendum in manner to be here- after prescribed. •

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.—The FINANCE COMMITTEE of the Corporation invite TENDERS from Local Printers and Stationers for the supply of PRINTING and STATIONERY required by the various departments of the Corporation during the next twelve months. Specifications, with conditions as to rates of wages and hours of labour, can be obtained on and after Monday next from the Borough Treasurer, Sankey Street, on deposit of 10s., which will be returned on receipt of a bona-fide tender. Tenders, securely fastened, addressed to the Chairman, of the Finance Committee, and endorsed " Tender for Printing and Stationery," must be delivered to theunder- signed on or before Saturday, the 19th October, 1912. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Hall Warrington. Town Clerk. 11th October, 1912.

CHILDREN ACT, 1908. (The Guardians are the Local Authority under Part I. of the Act.) NOTICE. INFANT LIFE PROTECTION. ANY PERSON HAVING THE CARE OF ANY INFANT under the age of Seven Years and who receives any reward for its nursing and maintenance MUST IMMEDI- ATELY REPORT such fact to the Officers of the District. (See below). Failure to report renders Persons liable to imprison- ment for a term not exceeeding six months, or to a fine not exceeding £25. Names and Addresses of Officers Under the Act F. GIDMAN, 99, Guardian-street, Warrington. THOS. BOWES, 65, Market-street, Earlestown. F. H. AUKLAND, 42, Church-street, Warrington. It. W. PEACOCK, 99, Guardian-street, Warrington. By Order, Bewsey Chambers, ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY, Warrington. Clerk to the Guardians.

TIOUSING, TOWN PLANNING &c., ACT, 1909. WARRINGTON. WHEREAS the Mayor, Aldermen and Burgesses of the County Borough of Warrington have applied to the Local Government Board under Part II of the Housing, Town Planning &c., Act, 1909, for authority to prepare a town planning scheme with reference to certain lands situate within the said Borough, in regard to which , notices of intention to make such application have been given in accordance with the Town Planning Procedure Regulations (England and Wales), 19 10 : AND WHEREAS the Local Government Board have directed a local inquiry to be held into the subject matter of such application: NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that Thomas Adams, Esq., who has been appointed to hold the Inquiry, will attend for that purpose at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Wednesday, the sixteenth day of October, 1912, at ten o'clock in the Forenoon and will then and there be Prepared to receive the evidence of any persons inter- ested in the matter of the said Inquiry. Local Government Board, NOEL T. KERSHAW. 2nd October, 1912. Assistant Secretary.
